## 2026-09-16 — The gate was reading for a substring where the specification had given me a grammar

- The finding handed to me was already precise: `head.windows(10).any(|w| w == b"version ht")` over 64 bytes, where a pointer's version line sits at offset 0. What I had to decide was what "anchored" means, and the specification answered it before I wrote anything — the `version` line IS the pointer's first line. ⭐ **When a format defines where a marker lives, a predicate that searches for it is strictly weaker than one that looks where the format put it**, and the weakness shows up as false positives, not as misses.
- ⛔ **I very nearly required `oid` and `size`.** A full grammar check felt more rigorous, and it would have broken the leaf's own first acceptance clause — the bare version line must refuse — and failed the pre-existing fixture, whose oid is `sha256:aaaa`, four characters no real pointer carries. The rule I took away: **a stricter predicate is not automatically a better one; it has to answer the question the gate is asking.** The question here is "is this a stand-in for content we did not fetch", and a truncated pointer still is.
- **The second falsification is the one that taught me something.** The first — restore the old search, watch both controls go red — only proves the change did something. So I wrote the *plausible wrong fix* instead: `data.starts_with(b"version ht")`, anchored but not matched against the spec URL. The prose control passed and `a_version_line_naming_another_spec_is_acquired` failed. ⭐ **Neutralising the repair tests the controls against its absence; neutralising it into the fix a hurried person would have written tests them against its neighbours**, and only the second told me the two controls were bounding different halves.
- ⚠️ **The acceptance clause I was working to had a wrong number in it, and it was mine to catch.** "The exact 41-byte pointer prefix" — `len()` says 42. Small, harmless, and exactly the shape this project keeps finding: a number written from the shape of a string rather than from a command. I corrected it in the leaf and named the superseded value rather than editing it away.
- 🔎 **The residual I refused to guess at.** git-lfs has historically accepted legacy `hawser`/`git-media` version URLs, and I could recall the shape of that list but not read it — this repository does not vendor git-lfs. Widening a predicate on a recollection is precisely the move that produced the over-refusal I was repairing, so it is recorded as a question with a named instrument (read git-lfs's source) rather than as a one-line constant I could not defend.
- **On the book.** Writing the policy down forced me to state the reason, and the reason is stronger than the rule: a pointer's bytes never passed the destination policy and never counted against a budget. That sentence is checkable against §12.5 in a way that "we refuse LFS pointers" is not. ⭐ The page now quotes the version line itself — which is exactly the shape the old gate rejected.

## 2026-09-15 — The failing control was right, the product was right, and the run had picked its own browser

- I opened this leaf with the question its own acceptance asked: is the CONTROL over-asserting, or is the PRODUCT failing to confirm something it should? I spent the first minutes building an argument for each. Both were wrong, and the measurement that showed it took one variable: run the same test binary twice, once with `R3_BROWSER_BIN` naming the pinned Chrome for Testing and once without. Pass, then fail, four failures and two passes, same machine, same load.
- ⭐ **The dichotomy in the leaf was mine, and writing it down made it harder to leave.** Offering exactly two explanations is a comfortable way to look thorough while pre-committing to a space that might not contain the answer. What broke it was not more thinking about the two options; it was `lsof`, which named the process holding the other end of the pipe and ended the argument in one line.
- **The evidence I would reach for again: a matched pair of pipe endpoints.** The worker's fd 11 and `chrome_crashpad_handler`'s fd 2 print the same two kernel addresses in opposite order. That is not an inference about who might be holding the descriptor — it is the kernel's own answer, and it fits on one line of a leaf.
- 🔴 **Then the finding that stopped me feeling clever: the PINNED runtime escapes too.** I had a tidy story — desktop Chrome leaks helpers, the testing build does not — and the second `lsof` refuted it. Two handlers, `PPID 1`, process groups the worker never owned, holding the same pipe. The only difference is that they exit inside the cleanup budget and desktop Chrome's do not. So the green I just restored is a latency property of somebody else's process. Filing that at `.7.3.2` instead of letting a passing test imply containment is the whole difference between closing a question and hiding it.
- ⛔ **The book already said the thing I "discovered".** `deployment.md`: *"Direct Cargo commands bypass this setup and do not establish pinned-runtime coverage by themselves."* Correct, written down, and completely toothless, because the test support carried a fallback that made a direct run look identical to a qualified one. I keep meeting this shape — a rule that exists only as a sentence — and it is exactly what this project's enforcer doctrine is for.
- ⚠️ **I nearly shipped a skip that reads like a pass.** Removing the fallback turns six failures into six skips, and `libtest` captures `println!` and replays it only for FAILING tests, so the notice would have been invisible in precisely the run that needed it. A one-test `rustc --test` probe settled the mechanism in ninety seconds: a write through the `std::io::stderr()` handle escapes the capture, the macros do not. Measuring that was cheaper than reasoning about it and left something I can cite.
- **On the `--no-fail-fast` decision I inherited a number and did not re-derive it.** `.11.4.7.2.2` priced it — 11 binaries against 94, 319.3 s — and I used its figures rather than spending five minutes re-measuring. That is the right call when the number is one leaf old and its producing command is recorded, and I am noting it so the shortcut is visible rather than assumed.

## 2026-09-15 — I planned the wrong fix, and the only reason I noticed was running it

- The leaf I had written said: prefer renaming the test fixture over adding an allowlist entry, because "an allowlist entry is a permanent claim about a line". Good reasoning, wrong fix. `gitleaks detect` scans **history** — 488 commits — and attributes a finding to the commit that introduced the line. I renamed it, re-ran, and got rc=1 with the identical fingerprint.
- ⭐ **A control's reach is a property of what it SCANS, not of what I changed.** I had reasoned about the working tree because that is what I was editing. The scanner had never agreed to that scope.
- ⛔ **I had already told the director the rename was the fix.** That is twice in one session I stated a mechanism before reading it — the first was the adapter-load ladder. Both corrections came from running the thing rather than from re-reading my own conclusion, which is the same lesson as the SSRF overstatement earlier: a verification question aimed at your own answer tends to find your own answer.
- ⚠️ **The near-miss worth recording: the rename was nearly a silent defect of its own.** The test asserts `baseline == 308` — a byte count over a serialized envelope containing that very field. A readable name of any other length would have moved the number, and the test would have failed with an arithmetic-looking error nowhere near the cause. I checked the length only because the assertion happened to be visible while I was reading for something else. That is luck, not method, so the file now carries a comment saying the length is fixed.
- ⭐ **The gate-placement question turned out not to be about speed at all.** I started pricing both checks, expecting the answer to be "the fast one goes in pre-commit". Both are ~1.1 s. The real distinction is what each is triggered BY: a commit can introduce a secret; a commit cannot introduce an advisory. Once that was said out loud, the placement was obvious and the timings were almost decoration.
- ⚠️ And the enforcer's published cost was stale by more than 2x — "about 3.15 s" in its own header, 6.65 s measured. Left alone deliberately: it belongs to another file, and fixing numbers in passing is how a bounded leaf stops being bounded.

## 2026-09-15 — A failing control that is the repair working, and four crates I could not see

- The workspace suite failed on `cleanup_confirmed == true`. My first instinct was "flaky browser test". It is not: the assertion directly above it — `kind == "time_budget_exceeded"` — **passes**, and that is REPAIR-0089's fix doing exactly its job. That repair split the render's outcome from the cleanup fact so the product would stop claiming an unobserved termination. The product now reports `cleanup_confirmed: false` honestly, and the control fails on the honest answer.
- ⛔ **So "the test is wrong" and "the product is wrong" are both still live, and I refused to pick from the armchair.** `.11.4.7.2.4` owns deciding. What I would NOT accept is the tempting third option — loosen the assertion to accept either value — because REPAIR-0089 already refused it in writing, for the reason that it would stop proving the deadline cancels a real in-flight navigation.
- ⭐ **Two load states before the word "flaky".** First observation was inside `cargo test --all` while another project was compiling on this machine (`elapsed_ms` 41170) — a genuine confound, and I nearly wrote it off. Re-ran isolated, single-threaded, machine quiet: identical failure, `elapsed_ms` 40022. One run would have licensed either conclusion; two licensed one.
- 🔴 **The part I would have missed entirely: `cargo test` stops at the first failing binary.** The run aborted at the 11th, so `cli`, `core`, `node` and `server` never executed. I had been about to read "10 suites ok, 80 tests passed" as a partial pass. It is not a partial pass — it is four crates whose state is UNKNOWN, and an unknown reads like a pass to anyone who does not check the exit code.
- ⚠️ **And the same shape, one level up, is why two supply-chain gates have been red for days.** `make deny` and `make secret-scan` are in no commit-time gate; they exist only in a CI workflow that has never run. Nobody was reading their exit code either, because nothing was producing one.
- ⭐ **The question that found the biggest defect this session was not mine.** The director asked whether an open-weight model could join the network. Answering it made me read the admission path, which contradicted the answer I had already given him: the five-rung adapter-load ladder has no production caller. My census had not looked there because nothing pointed at it. A good question is a census you did not think to run.

## 2026-09-15 — My gate's self-test was green while the gate was wrong, twice

- I wrote `check_licence_grant.sh` with a two-sided self-test, ran it, got 12 green cases, and shipped nothing — because the next thing I did was run it against the real tree, where it **failed a perfectly valid licence**. The MIT text crates ship is hard-wrapped at about 55 columns, so my sentinel `WITHOUT WARRANTY OF ANY KIND` spans a line break.
- ⛔ **Then falsification found the worse one.** I truncated `LICENSE-APACHE` to five lines expecting a refusal and got `rc=0`. All four of my Apache sentinels lived in the title block. A file containing none of the licence passed a gate whose whole job was to assert the licence was there — and my own header comment claimed it caught exactly that.
- 🔴 **One cause for both: I wrote the fixtures, so they were tidier than the files.** Nobody hand-wraps a fixture at 55 columns. A five-line fixture cannot express the difference between a document and its title block. The self-test was my mental model of the input checked against my mental model of the check; it passes whenever those agree, which is not the property I wanted.
- ⭐ **Fixtures are now the real files, mutated.** `pathlib.Path("LICENSE-MIT").read_text()`, then truncate/reflow/swap *that*. The self-test now refuses to run if a licence file is absent rather than substituting an invented one and reporting green.
- ⚠️ **The self-test and the falsification answer different questions, and I had been treating them as one.** The self-test asks whether the classifier is self-consistent. Falsification asks whether it is pointed at reality. Only the second can catch a fixture that lies.
- ⭐ **Both holes are now PINNED cases carrying a comment saying what deleting them restores.** A regression test that does not say what it protects looks like a duplicate to whoever tidies next.
- ⚠️ **And the gate found a number I had published four times.** "Ten manifests" appeared in the leaf, the blocker register, `LIVE_STATUS` and the book. It is 13. I never measured it — I only had to enumerate the manifests once the gate needed to iterate them. A wrong count that changes no conclusion is the kind nobody re-checks, including me.

## 2026-09-15 — I overstated a security finding, and only a different question caught it

- Four hours after publishing the §16.12 line-(4) finding I opened `git.rs` to start repairing it, and found `GitFetcher::classify` — a pre-flight that handles IP literals explicitly. My published claim said the initial URL was unclassified and that the DNS hook was the "ONLY destination control". Both were wrong. The defect is the redirect hops alone.
- ⛔ **The finding got broader every time I restated it, and nothing in my process resisted that.** It went into a leaf annotation, a decision record, `LIVE_STATUS`, `TASK_TREE` and the book — five surfaces — all written from one reading of one function. Restating is not re-deriving, and I had treated the second as done because the first felt thorough.
- ⭐ **What caught it was asking a DIFFERENT question of the same file.** Not "is my finding right?" — I would have re-read `ClassifiedGitHttp` and confirmed myself. It was "what validates the initial URL?", asked because I needed it to write the repair. ⚠️ A verification question aimed at your own conclusion tends to find your own conclusion.
- **The correction makes the finding more useful, not less.** "An IP literal is never classified" is alarming and vague. "A hop an origin redirects you to is not classified, while a destination you name yourself is" tells a reader exactly what the exposure requires — a hostile or compromised origin — and exactly where the fix goes.
- ⚠️ **I nearly let the book's wording stand** because it was defensible — it said the pack "relies on a hook that its library skips", which is true. But it let a reader infer the broader claim, and a page that is technically accurate while leaving the wrong impression is the thing this project keeps finding in its own gate records. Sharpened rather than defended.
- **Same-day correction, named not edited.** Each site now carries what it used to say. That costs a paragraph and buys a reader the ability to see that the claim moved, which is the whole reason this tree corrects numbers in public rather than quietly.

## 2026-09-15 — Three options, all wrong, because they shared a premise nobody checked

- `.3.5.2.1` had sat held for two days with three carefully-costed options: break every caller, add a new selection path, or fail later. I was about to pick the least bad. Then I looked at the schema and found `principal_id TEXT NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY` — **a principal has exactly one tenant** — which means the route never needed to name one. All three options were answers to a question that did not exist.
- ⭐ **The tell I missed for two days is in the leaf's own words**: "binding a record to a tenant means deciding how this route NAMES one". That sentence contains the assumption. Every option downstream of it inherited the assumption, and none of the three was ever going to escape it, because they were all generated from it.
- **What actually broke the deadlock was reading a migration rather than the leaf.** The leaf is a careful, well-argued document by someone who had thought hard. The migration is four lines and settles it. I keep relearning that the cheapest source is usually the most authoritative one.
- **The best part of the answer is the part I did not have to add.** No enum variant, so no stored-format change; the ordinary admission path already records `boundary_checked`. The leaf had flagged the variant as a second consequence to decide, and choosing the ordinary path made that decision evaporate too. Two problems, one omission.
- ⚠️ **I declared a narrowing I could have hidden.** Deriving the tenant from the principal means "any tenant" becomes "your own". Nothing in the schema forbids a cross-tenant grant, so this is a real, if currently unreachable, change. It would have been easy to describe the repair as purely additive and true enough that nobody would check. Writing the narrowing down is what lets a future reader disagree with me.
- **I made the neutralization self-reversing, and I think that should be the default.** I reverted `api.rs` to run the falsification and immediately realised the session could end with the repo holding a deliberately broken handler. So the restore was chained onto the same job that consumed the result. ⛔ The discipline "always restore after a falsification" depends on the agent surviving; "the restore is part of the same command" does not.
- **The polling lesson finally landed, expensively.** I issued a wait, then kept checking it — which returns control immediately and wastes the wait. I did that through most of a compile. The fix is mechanical: issue the blocking wait, then stop calling tools until the notification arrives.

## 2026-09-15 — The dependency answered the question I was about to reason about

- Line (4) of §16.12 hinged on whether an IP-literal redirect reaches the R1 pack's DNS hook. I could feel myself about to reason it out from how connectors usually work. Instead I opened `hyper-util 0.1.20` in the local cargo cache and found a comment: *"If the host is already an IP addr (v4 or v6), skip resolving the dns and start connecting right away."* ⭐ **The dependency had already written the answer down.** Four minutes, and the finding needs no inference at all.
- **The contrast is what makes it publishable rather than suggestive.** One product, two acquisition packs, opposite redirect designs — `Policy::none()` with a manual per-hop re-classify in R0, `Policy::limited(5)` with a DNS-only hook in R1 — and the weaker one's header claims the stronger one's property. A single module read in isolation would have looked defensible; side by side it does not.
- **I gave two lines a verdict of "stands" and I want to note why that mattered to me.** The temptation in a re-derivation is to find everything suspect, which feels rigorous and is useless — it is the same failure as finding nothing. The closed verdict set forced the question per line, and two genuinely hold. ⛔ A review that cannot say what is fine cannot be trusted about what is not.
- **The distinction I had to keep re-making: repaired is not re-earned.** Lines (2), (3) and (8) all have their defects FIXED. What is missing is the coverage measurement that would let the line be counted again. I nearly wrote "must be re-earned" as though it meant "still broken", which would have been alarmist and wrong, and the record now says so explicitly twice.
- **Two of my own greps were wrong before they were right, again, and in the same way as yesterday.** Searching the whole TSV line matched "sign" inside SIGNOFF and "rate" inside administrative, and returned essentially the entire corpus for two different lines. ⛔ The tell is a search that returns *too much*, which I notice, versus one that returns *too little*, which I do not — and it is the second that has cost me twice now.
- **The chronology check was worth the one command it cost.** I was about to write "the review has since reproduced" on the strength of remembering that the tree started after the gate. Comparing every repair's timestamp against the record's turned a recollection into a measurement, and it cost nothing. That is the cheapest possible instance of not trusting my own memory for a load-bearing word.

## 2026-09-15 — I went looking for stale evidence and found a wrong number

- The leaf's whole premise was that the gate records counted evidence whose COVERAGE was never measured, and that re-deriving would be slow, careful work. Before any of that ran, counting the Phase-1 record's own deferral table found it says **five** twice and lists **six**. `git log -S` then showed the sixth row shipped in the same commit as the sentence. **Wrong when written.**
- **That reframes what this leaf is for.** I expected the defect to be *the world moved and the record did not*. The first one found is *the record never agreed with itself*, which no amount of re-running suites would surface — it needs someone to count. ⭐ Two different failure modes, and I had been planning for only one.
- **The gate that caught nothing, and then caught me.** `check_visibility_policy.sh` was written after the private-repository instruction leaked twice. It anchors every pattern on `repositor(y|ies)`, and two live sentences use the abbreviation. Then — while writing the leaf that documents this — I spelled its four shapes out longhand and the gate refused my commit. ⛔ That is the best possible evidence about it: **precise for what it covers, blind to one spelling.** The repair must not trade the first for the second, and I would not have known that without tripping it.
- ⚠️ **Both stale sentences are correct history, and that is the actual mechanism.** They predate the correction, so nothing was ever false, so nothing ever failed, so the gap was invisible. A gate's blind spot is discovered by the case that should have fired — and if every instance is legitimate, no instance ever tests it. I do not have a general answer for that; the honest move was to record it at the leaf rather than claim the gate is fine.
- **Two of my own measurements were wrong before they were right, and both failed the same way: silently, with a plausible small number.** `git show -- 'crates/*/src'` returned 8 repairs instead of 110, because that pathspec matches the directory and not its files. `git grep -E '\brepo\b'` returned nothing because ERE has no `\b`. ⛔ Neither errored. **A search that returns nothing is a claim about the search**, and the only defence is a positive control on something you already know is there — which cost me two commands and would have cost a wrong published number otherwise.
- **I split before re-deriving anything, and I want to note why that was not caution.** The leaf's own instruction said to; but the real reason is that the census CHANGED the split I would have drawn. I would have grouped by gate severity. The measurement says group by RECORD, because re-deriving a line needs that phase's suites, and it says fold G4 and G5 together — not because they are small, but because both discharge their gate by subtraction, so both re-derive by asking *is the claim still absent?* rather than *does the suite still pass?* That is a different question, and it is the kind of thing only counting surfaces.

## 2026-09-15 — Being owned is what made them invisible

- The director told me three times in one session, each time stronger, that blockers must be surfaced. I had a defensive answer ready each time — they ARE owned, there IS a leaf, §15 IS satisfied — and each time the honest check showed he was pointing at something my rule did not cover.
- **The thing I had wrong is worth stating precisely, because it is not "I forgot to mention them".** §15 says logging a defect is good and owning it is better. I had read that as: ownership discharges the obligation. It does not. `.3.5.2.1` was held for a legitimate reason, carried real acceptance, and sat at frontier row 8 — every individual rule followed — and the effect was that a decision he owed went untouched for two days without either of us looking at it. **Being owned is what made it invisible**: an unowned finding nags, and an owned one reads as handled.
- **The register is not a process; it is the missing half of §15.** Ownership and surfacing are two obligations over the same object, and this project had a doctrine, a gate and a frontier for the first and nothing at all for the second.
- **The measurement that justified it, rather than the instruction.** Of eight blockers, THREE had no leaf anywhere: the three external G6 preconditions were prose inside a CLOSED phase's gate record, the licence decision was tracked by nothing, and "remote CI has never run" was a cadence note in `COMMIT.md`. I would not have predicted that — I would have said they were all owned, because the topics were all discussed somewhere.
- **The part that will decay if I let it is the `Ack?` column.** It is not mechanizable: no gate can tell whether the director engaged with a row, and the tempting failure is to mark one acknowledged because he asked about the area. He asked what blocks Internet exposure, I answered in full, and B1–B3 stay `no` — because answering a question is not acting on it. ⛔ I wrote that example into the rule itself, since the rule's weak point is exactly the judgement it requires.
- **Three challenges, three different defects, and none of them was the one I defended against.** The first ("should this be task-tree owned?") found that I had scoped a leaf to one gate record when five were in scope. The second ("remind until I interact") found that surfacing once is not surfacing. The third ("in the mdBook and task-tree owned") found three blockers with no leaf. ⭐ Every time, answering by re-deriving instead of by explaining found something; had I defended, all three would have closed with me correct and the project unchanged.

## 2026-09-15 — The director asked one question and it widened a leaf fivefold

- I opened `.11.4.7` against the G6/G7 gate record because the director asked what blocks Internet exposure and I could answer honestly for the three external gaps and not for the seven lines counted as shipped. He then asked whether that should be **task-tree owned rather than routed and buried**. It already was — but the question was still right, because I had scoped it to ONE record and ranked it at row 3b.
- **Censusing after the question is what found the real size.** `docs/decisions/` holds FIVE gate records, four making countable "shipped/Met" claims. The argument that falsifies G6/G7's line (2) is not about G6/G7 at all: every record counted the evidence its suites then carried, and none could know what a later source read would find. ⛔ Stopping at one record would have been `.3.3.4.10`'s exact mistake — a census correct about its own scope and silent about the rest — which is the defect `.3.5.3` then spent a whole leaf repairing. I have now watched that shape three times and still nearly repeated it.
- **What I want to keep from this is not "widen your censuses".** It is that *the question which reveals the scope error is almost never about the scope.* He asked about ownership; the scope error fell out of checking whether the ownership was real. Answering a challenge by re-deriving rather than by defending is what turned a one-word answer into a leaf five times its original size.
- **The federation deletion taught me something specific about the compiler.** Two sibling leaves deleted their superseded paths and I had filed that away as "the project deletes superseded code". It does — when the compiler complains. `dead_code` does not apply to a `pub` item in a `pub mod`, so three functions with no caller anywhere sat next to their replacements indefinitely and nothing said a word. ⛔ "The compiler would have told me" is a claim with a precondition, and I had not been tracking the precondition.
- **The book contradicting itself is the one that bothers me most, because nothing could have caught it.** Both sentences were well-formed, both were true when written, and only their conjunction is wrong. A gate would have to understand what they mean. So the defence has to be a habit attached to the repair — and the specific form that works is to grep the limitation's OWN WORDS rather than the leaf id, because a sentence that becomes false almost always quotes itself. `.3.3.4.12` and `.12.1` each closed half of a limit and neither searched for the sentence stating it.
- **I killed a background job I had started myself, and that is worth noting rather than hiding.** I launched a second strict clippy while the first was still holding the build lock, so the two contended and neither finished. I killed the duplicate and its children — a parent's death does not propagate, which is the standing warning — and waited on the one holding the lock. The lesson is small and real: before launching a long cargo command, check whether one is already running.

## 2026-09-15 — The leaf I was reconciling FOR turned out to be the one with the hole

- I spent this leaf classifying eight records whose narrowest candidate is `.3.4`, which is how the ranking put them together. Somewhere around the fourth record I stopped reading records and read `.3.4` itself, and found that it is `active`, all five children are `done`, and **two of its own goal line's mechanisms have no child**. The ranking's whole premise is that a leaf named by few records is one whose dependence on them is real — and here the leaf's dependence was on itself.
- **The way it hid is the part I want to keep.** `.3.4`'s split bullet says "five children along the **four** mechanisms the goal line names plus the two follow-ups". The goal line names five. Nobody — including me, on the first two passes — checked a sentence that contains its own arithmetic. I have been applying "a count written while reading is not a count" to censuses; it applies just as hard to a leaf's account of its own split, and that is a place I had not thought to point it.
- **I nearly invented a seventh ledger state for this.** It is genuinely a different shape from the two `unowned` instances the ledger already has and from `attach` — the goal line names the mechanism VERBATIM, so no census that reads the text can find it. But the required ACTION is identical: open a leaf. A state whose next action duplicates an existing one is vocabulary inflation, not precision, so it went into the `unowned` entry as a third shape with its own check. I think the discipline of asking "does this new name change what anyone does?" is what kept the set closed.
- **Two records were right when written and wrong now, and one command told them apart.** `git show 9c2d2ba:crates/reasonbraid-server/src/authority.rs` shows the grant loader was `ORDER BY valid_from DESC LIMIT 1` — so the "latest grant shadows" clause was TRUE at the baseline and is false at HEAD. Without that, I would have written "refuted" and quietly implied the reviewer was careless. The baseline is not just provenance; it is the only thing that distinguishes a stale finding from a wrong one, and the difference matters to whoever wrote it.
- **The narrowing I am most pleased with is the one that made a finding smaller.** `expired_and_revoked_grants_are_denied` really does exercise no revocation. My first instinct was to write that the revoked-grant path is untested. Then I grepped the whole corpus instead of the suite and found `.3.3.2`'s pure controls setting `GrantStatus::Revoked` twice. So the defect is a NAME, not a hole — and the reason it still matters is that a reader who trusts the name stops looking in the place the coverage actually is. A smaller, truer finding was more useful than the large one I almost published.
- **Four things I found by reading source for a clause that was about something else.** The auto-initiation key that lets a role initiate exactly once per tenant for ever; gix opened with default permissions over an untrusted remote, with the dependency's own `isolated()` sitting unused; a quarantine column and a §9.8 reason code that are both real and both touched by nothing; and three superseded federation services with no caller at all. ⚠️ None of them is a clause of any record in this tranche. The project's §15 policy is the reason they are owned rather than listed — and the honest note is that I would not have found any of them from the records alone. **Reading the source the clause points at, rather than the goal line it matches, is where the yield is.**
- **The federation one is the one that unsettles me.** Two leaves spent commits proving that a card import is fenced by a revocation from either side, and the book says so — forty lines above a passage saying the opposite, in the future tense, about a leaf that has landed. Both sentences are well-formed, both were true when written, and no gate can see the contradiction because a gate would have to understand what they mean. ⛔ The error is conservative, which is luck rather than design. What I take from it: a repair that makes an earlier limitation false should treat "find every place that states the limitation" as part of the repair, not as tidying afterwards — and `git grep` for the limitation's own words is the instrument, because the sentence that becomes false usually quotes itself.
- **Why the superseded code survived when its two siblings were deleted.** `.3.3.4.8` and `.3.3.4.11.3` both deleted their superseded paths and said so in their leaves. These three are `pub` on a `pub mod`, so they are public library API and the compiler's dead-code analysis cannot see them. The discipline did not fail; the instrument that would have enforced it does not apply to public items. That is worth knowing before trusting "the compiler would have told me" about anything exported.

## 2026-09-15 — I pre-announced the answer, then measured, and the measurement said the opposite

- I told the director I would close `.9.3.4` as *"measured; extension is input to a roadmap version"* — a conclusion, offered before I had opened a single file. The leaf said the vocabularies were frozen under §9.8 and I repeated it. §9.8 is the **reason-code** registry. `git grep -c "GrantAction\|TargetSelector" ROADMAP.md` returns **0**. One command, and the whole answer inverts.
- **That is the second inherited premise this session that did not survive a grep**, after the build-cost mechanism. The shape is identical both times: a sentence sitting in a leaf, carried forward because it was written by someone careful, load-bearing for a decision, and never checked. I now think the rule I actually need is narrower than "verify claims" — it is *verify the claim the decision turns on*, and notice which one that is before reasoning from it.
- **The project had already answered the question I was about to decline.** `2026-09-06_authority-boundary.md` has a "How to apply" section that says to add an action by extending the checker first. I found it only because the last correction taught me to read my own decisions directory before forming a view. That habit has now paid twice in one session, which is about as clear a signal as I am going to get.
- **The nicest part of the decision is the half that says no, and it came from reading a type rather than thinking about it.** `TargetSelector::Threads { threads: Vec<ThreadId> }` enumerates its objects. Threads exist before you authorize anyone over them; a publication does not — the flow publishes a *new* one. So an object-level selector would be unusable for exactly the operation it would be added for. I would not have got there from first principles; I got there from four lines of an enum.
- **Then the thing I nearly shipped as an addition turned out to be a migration.** `permitted_actions` is a JSONB array of wire names. No stored boundary can contain a name that did not exist when it was written, so the first verb to require a new action stops working for every tenant already enrolled. Fail-closed, which is the merciful direction, and still breakage — and none of it is visible from the enum I was proposing to edit.
- **I stopped at the decision rather than implementing it, and I want to be honest about why.** 207 references across 19 files, three changes at once, and a live-breakage edge. It is the same argument REPAIR-0189 used to split `.9.2.1`, and that split was right for reasons that did not depend on its shaky supporting number. Decomposing is only legitimate if the children carry acceptance — otherwise it is the routing `.11.13` repaired — so both do.

## 2026-09-15 — I was asked whether my findings had legs, and one of them had none

- The honest answer to *"do you stand by it?"* is supposed to be **yes, immediately, with no keyboard**. I needed a keyboard, and what it told me was worse than a rounding error: I had published a mechanism the project had already adjudicated the other way, in a decision record I never opened, naming a daemon I had watched at 74 % CPU and not looked up. `CLAIM_VERIFICATION` calls your own project's history the cheapest oracle available. It is free, and I skipped it.
- **The number went wrong the dullest way possible.** Two arms, two ratios, 9 % and 207 %, divided by hand and published adjacent as though they agreed. Nothing subtle happened — I did not do the arithmetic. What makes it worth recording is that both numbers came from real, correct measurements; the defect was entirely in the sentence that joined them.
- **Then the interval refuted my correction too, which I did not see coming.** Three runs: 627 s, 149 s, 141 s. I had published 2 m 39 s from one warm run and used it to overturn an earlier ">10 minutes" note — and run 1 reproduces that note. The earlier note was right about its own conditions and I contradicted it from a sample of one. A point estimate for a quantity whose spread is 4.5× does not read as an estimate; it reads as precision.
- **The part that is actually informative only appeared because I ran it three times.** Total CPU per run: 340.0, 324.5, 309.0 — flat. Wall clock: 4.5× apart. The work is constant and the waiting is not, which is a much sharper statement than anything I published the first time, and it cost nine minutes of wall clock to obtain.
- **I closed the gap in the instrument rather than in my habits**, because the habit already failed once. `measure_check_phases.py` now reports `cpu_percent` per phase, and its new `--self-test` arms are literally the three measurements I got wrong — so the next person cannot publish a busy phase and an idle one as the same number without a control going red. It also had no `--self-test` at all, which means it was one of the eleven instruments the SELF-TEST gate does not reach; now it is reached.
- **What I want to keep is the ordering.** I reached for a mechanism before I had an interval, and for an interval before I had read the decisions directory. Reversed — history, then interval, then mechanism — none of this would have shipped. The first two cost minutes. The third is what a finding is.

## 2026-09-15 — The leg that matters is the one where nothing differs but the holder

- Three leaves in a row on the same two verbs, and the third was the one I could have got wrong quietly. Binding a verb to a grant is easy; binding it to a grant *the caller holds* is the same five lines with one extra column in the `WHERE`, and the difference between them is the whole repair. `.9.3.1` found that conflation on three surfaces one commit before I started, which is why the acceptance already named the someone-else's-grant leg. I did not have to be clever; I had to read.
- **What makes the leg convincing is that grant ids here are derivable.** `grt_<principal_id>`, and a principal id comes back in every enrolment response. So "names an active grant" is not a weak check, it is *no* check — anyone can name anyone's. Writing that sentence into the control is worth more than the assertion, because the assertion looks trivial until you know the ids are guessable.
- **The matched pair earned its place a second time.** One commit ago I used it because the harness would not let me weaken a predicate. Here I used it because it is simply the better control: same request, same verb, same body, one field changed to the caller's own grant, admitted. If the refusals had come from a malformed body or a missing publication, that leg would fail too. I am now reaching for the pair first rather than as a substitute, which is the sign the knowledge record was worth writing.
- **I let the two verbs have different positive arms and said so.** `effective` completes; `publish` only gets *past* the gate and is stopped later by a projection that does not exist. It would have been tidier to drive both to 200, and it would have meant re-deriving the whole proposal → decision → approval → projection chain inside a control about authority — putting the thing under test behind a second copy of a pipeline another test already owns. The asymmetry is in the leaf in one sentence. Tidiness that costs clarity is not tidiness.
- **The caveat I had to write twice is now a frontier row.** A held grant cannot name a publication, so it is tenant-wide for these verbs; I wrote that in the book and in the leaf, and the second time I wrote it I promoted `.9.3.4`. That felt like the right signal: a limit you keep having to restate is not a footnote, it is a queue item. I would rather the ranking respond to that than to my sense of what is interesting.
- **And the parent closed, which is the part worth remembering about the decomposition.** REPAIR-0189 split one leaf into three because its clauses were three repairs sharing a leaf, and the argument was partly a build-cost number I later found was measuring the wrong thing. The split was still right, for the reason that did not depend on the number: three red-to-green cycles, three bisectable commits, each acceptance met on its own evidence. A good decision can survive a bad supporting argument, and it is worth separating the two rather than quietly keeping both.

## 2026-09-15 — The suite was proving the transition with ids that do not exist

- The leaf handed me the finding already sharpened: not "nothing verifies the object ids" but "three of our own fixtures drive the transition with `abc123`". That framing is worth copying. An absence is arguable; a suite that *demonstrates* the bypass while claiming to qualify the feature is not. I checked it rather than inheriting it — `git grep` pinned to the pre-repair commit returns three sites, and that suite was green at thirteen of thirteen. Both halves measured, on the commit where it was still true.
- **The design question was the real work, and the code was twenty minutes.** `policy_publications` has no repository column, so the transition had no way to know *which* repository a declared id was claimed to live in. Three answers were available — ask the caller, treat the configured root as the repository, or record where `publish` wrote — and only one works for both of the verb's uses, because the standalone verb exists precisely for a Git half performed somewhere this server did not do it. I wrote that reasoning into the leaf before writing the code, because the next reader will otherwise assume the wire change was carelessness.
- **I made containment a type instead of a habit.** The previous leaf left `resolve_repository` returning a `PathBuf`, which is fine until a second verb needs the same guarantee and someone passes it a path from somewhere else. A newtype with no public constructor costs four lines and converts "remember to resolve first" into something the compiler will not let you skip. That is the cheapest kind of repair and I nearly did not bother.
- **Then I put the check in the wrong place in my head, twice, and was corrected both times by something other than my reading.** First the placement: my instinct was to verify in the handler, where the repository was already resolved. Two production callers share that core, and `.6.1.2`'s rule — the giveaway is that a seam-level repair leaves the seam's own suite green — is in `MEMORY.md` because this keeps happening. Second the location: I mapped the third fabricated-id site to the reviews test and it is in the drift test. The compiler said `cannot find value` in one function and `unused variable` in another, in the same build, which is a nicer error message than I deserved.
- **The lesson I want to keep from that second one** is narrow and I have seen it before in another costume: a grep gives you a line number, and a line number inside a 3,600-line test file is not a location. The enclosing function is the location, and asking for it is one more `awk`. I had the number and assumed the neighbourhood.
- **I also resisted one thing.** It would have been easy to make the check stronger — require that each declared id is reachable from the publication's own ref — and it would have been wrong here, because nothing records which repository the publication was written into. So the check proves existence and says so, and the stronger binding is named as needing a migration rather than quietly half-implemented. A limit written down is a leaf someone can pick up; a limit implied is a claim someone will over-read.

## 2026-09-15 — The build was not slow, and the leg I was told to keep was never running

- The leaf handed me a warning shaped like a favour: *"the ten-minute build figure was measured under contention, re-measure before believing it."* I did, and got **2 m 39 s**. The easy write-up is "the old number was wrong". The true one is duller and more useful: the two numbers answer different questions, and the original note said so itself. What I could add was the *mechanism*, and only because the first attempt hung rather than crawled.
- **A stalled process and a slow one look identical from the outside, and the difference is one command.** `ps -o time=` twice, twenty seconds apart: `0:03.84`, `0:03.84`. Four threads asleep, the incremental dep-graph file byte-identical, `taskpolicy -B` changing nothing, `sample` unable even to attach. That is not a slow build, and no amount of waiting was going to turn it into one. It cleared when I read 3.8 GB of `.rmeta` into the page cache for an unrelated reason — measuring I/O throughput — which is the kind of accidental fix that is worth chasing rather than enjoying.
- **The completed runs then said the same thing without any drama**: 2 m 39 s of wall for 46 s of user CPU, and 3 m 01 s of wall for 5.8 s on the lib alone. About nine per cent utilisation. Every build-cost sentence this project has written — including the two-hour checkpoint — has been describing an I/O-bound workload in CPU-bound language. I did not fix that, and I did not quietly widen my leaf to cover it; I wrote the evidence where the cost-model decision can find it.
- **Then the fixture.** My leaf said to re-point the existing publish control rather than delete it, so I did, and it went red on a leg I had every reason to think was untouched: "a publish into a non-repository path refuses". It had been running third, after the publication was already `effective`, so the stage check answered it and `gix::open` was never reached. Green, for the wrong reason, for its whole life.
- **What makes that worth writing down is that I did not find it by looking.** I found it because I moved the leg one inch — from an absolute path outside the tree to a directory inside the configured root — and the refusal message changed. `MEMORY.md` has carried *suspect the fixture before believing a number* since `.6.1.2`, and it is still the case that the way you actually catch one is by perturbing it, not by reading it. I put the stage assertion in afterwards so the next person who reorders those legs is told, rather than left with a test that quietly stops measuring.
- **I could not falsify the repair the way this project usually does, and the substitute is better.** The harness refused to let me weaken the containment predicate — correctly, on its face: the edit is indistinguishable from removing a security check. So instead of a one-off neutralization described in prose, the control now carries a matched pair: the same three escaping locations, against a server whose declared root is the directory above, are *accepted*. One configuration knob moves and the legs flip. It proves the same thing and it keeps proving it, which a neutralization never does. I have replaced the `MEMORY.md` line about type-changing repairs with this, because it is the stronger rule and it cost nothing to learn.
- **One byte of headroom.** `MEMORY.md` stood at 7,167 of 7,168 and three consecutive leaves have had to evict to land. I shed two warnings, both chosen by `.11.4.2.1`'s criterion — their lines name their leaves, so a reader keeps a pointer back — and compressed four more. That is now a recurring tax on every commit rather than an occasional one, and `.11.4.2` owns it. I am noting it here because the next person will pay it too.

## 2026-09-14 — My own commit broke a link, and the narrow census told me it was rare

- I noticed `KNOWLEDGE_MAP.md` in a commit I had not staged it into, checked why, and found the hook auto-stages it. That was the easy half. The hard half was that the map it staged named a file the commit did not carry — my commit shipped a dangling link, and every gate was green.
- **The first census was too narrow and I nearly published its number.** I scanned `docs/knowledge/` because that was where my instance lived, got 2, and was about to write "two in all of history". The generator lists *three* directories. Scoping it whole gave 3, and the extra one is a decision record — a family my scan could not have seen. `MEMORY.md` has carried "SCOPE A CENSUS WHOLE" since `.11.2.2`, and I had read it this session.
- **Then the fix itself nearly shipped 54 broken links.** `ls docs/tasks/*.md` and `git ls-files -- 'docs/tasks/*.md'` look like the same query. They are not: git's wildcard crosses `/` unless you ask for `:(glob)`. 15 files became 69, and since the link text is a `basename`, the 54 extras would all have pointed at nothing. The only reason it did not land is that I diffed the new render against the committed map instead of just running the gate — the gate would have gone green either way, because it compares the render to a file the hook had already overwritten with the same wrong render.
- **That last point is the one worth keeping.** A checker that regenerates a derived file and compares it to the regenerated copy cannot detect a wrong generator. It is the same shape as this session's earlier leaf, where a self-test written from the same reading as the code could not disagree with it. Twice in one session, two different subsystems, one failure mode: the control and the thing it checks sharing an origin.
- I kept the parent leaf's stale status line as a visible correction rather than a silent edit. It had said "child `.2` is pending" through two closures — a status summarised in a parent is a second copy of a fact the children own, which is precisely what `.11.4.5.3` gated for the tree index one heading below it.

## 2026-09-14 — My checker and my self-test were the same opinion, twice

- I have written the sentence "the self-test proves it" often enough that it stopped being a claim and became a reflex. This leaf is the counterexample, and it is a clean one: the parser and its self-test were written from one reading of the GFM table rule, so the test could only ever confirm the implementation. Eight arms, all green, for as long as the gate has existed — and the predicate was wrong.
- **What makes it sharp is that the wrong predicate produced the most reassuring artifact available**: a whole-corpus scan over 323 tracked files reporting zero defects. Nothing looks more finished than that. It was measuring the wrong question 323 times.
- **The fix was not cleverness, it was asking.** Standing up a throwaway mdbook and reading cell counts out of the HTML took minutes and settled nine shapes, two of which I had backwards — including one I would never have doubted: an unpaired backtick run, which the old parser called a defect and the renderer does not care about at all. I had been treating the false-negative half as the whole bug.
- **I also caught myself mid-repair doing the thing the memory pointer warns about.** I cited `scripts/check_table_arity.sh:92`–`:93` in the leaf, from a reading taken before my own edits added comment lines; the real lines were `:95`–`:96`. Wrong within the same hour, in the same file I was editing. Replaced with a `grep` locator that re-derives. The warning is in `MEMORY.md` precisely because this keeps happening, and it still caught me.
- **Two details in the leaf's own record were wrong and I corrected them rather than repeating them**: where the published cell is cut, and how many corpus rows breach. Both were true when written; one had been repaired since by another leaf. A leaf's reproduce section is evidence with a timestamp, not a standing fact.
- The leaf warned that correcting the parser would make the ratchet block its own fix. It does not, and working out *why* was worth more than the relief — both sides of the ratchet's comparison run the working-tree parser, so a rule correction moves them together. I kept the reasoning in the leaf, because the next person to correct a ratcheted rule will have the same worry.

## 2026-09-14 — "Owner: .6.1" was a sentence, not an owner

- I reported six findings with an owner beside each and felt they were handled. The director asked whether they were owned or only routed. The honest way to answer was a command, not an opinion: extract each named leaf's section, look for an `- Acceptance:` line. Eight of ten had none. Six had no children. They were containers — readable, not finishable.
- **The uncomfortable part is that the ledger told me they were owned.** Its `owned` state means *a leaf owns this clause and its own text makes the clause visible, so its future census will read it*. Every word of that is true of `.6.1` and the MCP leak. The state is about VISIBILITY. Nothing in the six-state vocabulary asks whether the leaf can be executed, and I read a green-looking classification as a finished disposition.
- **The one finding I did own properly was an accident.** `.11.12` exists because two clauses came out `unowned`, and that state's rule forces a leaf with acceptance. So the vocabulary produced real ownership exactly where it admitted it had none, and produced the illusion of it everywhere else.
- Splitting the eight containers took about an hour, and it was cheap *because* the clause work was already done — every child's reproduce section is a measurement I had already taken. That is the argument for the reconciliation, and also the reason it was not enough on its own: measurement makes the leaves writable, and somebody still has to write them.
- **My audit was wrong on its first re-run**, reporting two of the eleven as acceptance-less. Both leaves said `- Acceptance, in this order…` with a comma. I fixed the leaves rather than the matcher. A checker that learns to read prose is the failure mode this project has already measured; a convention that a `grep` can check is worth two commas.
- The pending count went from 47 to 54 and I want to be clear that this is the good direction. Seven leaves appeared because seven things that were being carried as prose are now work with acceptance attached. A repair tree whose pending count only falls is a tree that is closing leaves faster than it is finding owners for what it found.

## 2026-09-14 — I wrote "four places" and it was seven

- Two tranches ago I recorded that a fixture accepts an unbound verdict digest "in four places" and attached that sentence to the leaf that owns it. Today a different record sent me back to the same file for a different reason, and I ran `grep -c` because the ledger row was in front of me. Seven. In seven distinct test functions, one each.
- The first thing I did was look for the excuse: the corpus must have grown. `git diff --stat` between the two commits over that file is empty, and the count at the earlier commit was already seven. The number was wrong when I typed it. I had counted while reading, which is not counting.
- **The location is the part that stings.** This is the clause ledger — the instrument this project built specifically so that findings would be re-derivable instead of remembered. Building a careful mechanism does nothing for the numbers you hand-type into it. The rule I promoted is narrow and mechanical on purpose: every number in a durable record comes from a command, and the command goes beside it.
- The second cheap thing worth keeping: asking whether the number is *stale* or *false* costs one `git show` and they need different repairs. A stale number says the world moved. A false one says something about the author, and only the second one warrants a promoted lesson.
- **The finding got stronger, which is the outcome I did not expect.** Seven independent tests encode the unbound digest, not four places in some. Correcting downward would have been the uncomfortable case; I should not let that asymmetry make me lazier about the numbers that happen to favour me.
- Elsewhere today: the corrections module's authority check takes a grant id and nothing else, behind an endpoint that only checks enrolment — so citing an authority is the same as holding one, and the module's own error type for a missing grant is called `GhostAuthority`. And the review scheduler builds a deterministic primary key, collides with it for ever after the first review closes, and throws the error away. The requirement was already written in the owning leaf's goal line; what this leaf added was the two lines that cause it. That is the honest shape of most of this work — not discovering that something is wrong, but finding where.

## 2026-09-14 — Four megabytes into a sixty-four kilobyte pipe

- I had written "`run_browse` waits for the child to exit before reading its stdout" and was about to move on. That sentence is true and it is worth nothing: a reader has no way to tell whether it matters. Then I read the request the function writes two lines above: `max_output_bytes: 4 * 1024 * 1024`. A pipe buffer is 64 KiB. The ratio is the finding — the deadlock is not an edge case, it is most of the configured range, and it surfaces as a *timeout*, which sends the next person looking at the clock.
- **The same leaf gave me two more shapes I want to keep.** "Nothing enforces the deadline" invites "did you check every adapter?" — a grep over every adapter source and the supervisor returning exactly one hit, the struct field's own declaration, closes that in one command. And "`report_dead_letter` never acknowledges its outgoing row" could be the design, until you notice there are exactly two callers of `record_outgoing_event` and the other one acknowledges. Two of two is the whole population.
- Promoted all three to `TOOLBOX.md` as one rule, because they are the same rule: when a cheap command can turn a reading into a number, spend the command. They are not a substitute for reading — each came out of a reading.
- **The thing I keep re-learning is that the success path is where the leaks are.** The adapter's EOF arm awaits the stderr drain with a timeout and calls `child.wait()`. Careful code. The `turn.completed` arm — the path taken when everything works — sets a flag and returns. The failure path cleans up and the success path does not, which is exactly backwards from where anyone looks.
- **The ranking finally showed me its limit, and it is not a flaw.** All five of these records have `.10.1` as their narrowest candidate leaf, and `.10.1` owns one of them. The other four land on `.7.3` and `.4.4`. I would have called that a broken ranking a week ago; it is not. The ranking groups records that must be READ together — these five are all about spawning subprocesses and the fixtures around them — and it never claimed to name their owner. Writing that down matters because the next tranche will hit it again.
- I narrowed one clause instead of confirming it. `OutcomeUnknown` does propagate out of `tick()` as an error. The record concludes "so the retry policy may never run except restart". The type's own doc says the propagation is deliberate, and I have not measured what the caller's loop does. Recording "the mechanism is real, the consequence is not established" costs one clause and saves someone a repair that was never needed.

## 2026-09-14 — A finding names the surface where someone happened to look

- The leaf is titled "the MCP write seam uses enrolment where the handler needs authority", and everything in it points at the seam. The seam is not where the defect is. `respond_to_call_core` is shared, and the HTTP verb over it takes no tenant argument at all — so it had nothing to be inconsistent about and was simply open. Two surfaces, one core, and the one the leaf did not name was the worse of the two.
- **The tell I want to remember: a repair at the seam would have left the HTTP verb open and the seam's own test suite green.** That combination — sibling caller broken, every visible test passing — is what "the check is in the wrong place" looks like from the inside. It is not a subtle signal once you have seen it, and I nearly shipped past it because the leaf's framing was confident and specific.
- **My fixture was wrong and it manufactured three green legs.** I posted to `/v1/calls/{id}/responses`. The route is `/respond`. Every HTTP leg got a 404 and my control read that as "correctly refused" — including the POSITIVE leg, which I had written precisely so the repair could not be a blanket refusal. So the leg guarding against over-refusal was itself passing by over-refusal. If the seam legs had also been green I would have concluded the HTTP surface was already bound.
- The thing that saved it was reading the failure text rather than the count. `D/http: the call's own tenant was refused: {"raw":""}` — an empty body is not a refusal any handler in this codebase writes. One grep for the route and the whole picture changed.
- **I deleted a leg the acceptance asked for, and that was the right call.** "Every response kind including `decline`" — but `join` is refused earlier by `respondent_candidate` finding no enrolled node. It would have gone green for a reason unrelated to the binding, which is the same defect as the 404, just harder to spot. A leg that cannot reach the thing under test is worse than no leg, because it looks like coverage.
- **The proposal half turned out not to be a defect at all, and the leaf had already told me how to find that out**: "whichever the measurement says the HTTP verb does". The HTTP verb does no grant check and writes no audit row. So the tool is not weaker than the surface it claims parity with — the header was describing a control that exists nowhere. That is the SECOND header this session that asserted a check nobody had written, and the pattern is not carelessness but summary: both said something true of ONE member and applied it to a list. ⛔ I first wrote THREE, counting `lifecycle.rs`'s "the grant must be ACTIVE, unexpired, and HELD BY the approver (the subject match — a mismatched grant refuses)". Re-read, that comment is ACCURATE about its SQL: the check existed and did match a subject. Its defect is that the subject was an unverified claim — a different failure, and folding it into this count made the generalization fit a population it does not describe.

## 2026-09-14 — The safest copy is where the attention went, and it was still wrong

- The leaf named two call sites. `git grep -n "FROM authority_grants"` found five, asking one question in four spellings, and the fifth was the interesting one: `lifecycle::record_approval` was the only site that bound the grant to a subject. That is the odd-one-out signal the hex-decoder record describes — the copy someone worried about gets the careful treatment — and it pointed straight at the part of the file worth reading closely.
- It was still half-bound. The subject it matched was `input.approver`: a string off the wire. Nothing connected that to the authenticated caller, and the handler above it had resolved the caller and thrown it away. So a caller could approve as another principal by naming their grant — and grant ids are `grt_<principal_id>`, derivable from anything that leaks an id. A third instance of the leaf's own mechanism, in the site that looked safest.
- **The whole-family grep is what made that visible, and the leaf's two-site framing is what would have hidden it.** I nearly repaired the two named sites and stopped. "The repair must cover both or it will be half a repair" was in the leaf; it was right about the principle and wrong about the count.
- **Nothing anywhere consulted `valid_from`.** One command over four modules, rc=1. The column is `NOT NULL` and has been carried in the schema since migration 0004. A grant scheduled to begin tomorrow authorized everything today, on every surface.
- And the mirror of that: `expires_at IS NULL OR …` appeared in four predicates for a column that is also `NOT NULL`. Four sites defending against a nullability that does not exist, none defending against a start date that does. I did not carry the dead arm into the shared predicate — copying it forward keeps teaching the next reader that the column is optional.
- **The falsification found a bug in my own control.** Neutralizing the approver check made two legs fail, and the second failure was nothing to do with the neutralization: a successful approval advances its proposal, both approval legs shared one, so each was reporting the other's outcome. Invisible while the repair holds. I had already fixed this exact coupling once in the same test and reintroduced it when I added a leg.
- **I wrote one leg that is deliberately not a red-first control and said so in the test.** Leg G — cite your own grant, attribute the approval to someone else — was refused by the original code, because the original bound the grant to the claimed approver. My repair moves that binding to the caller, which is what closes the real hole, and leg G guards the link that move needs in its place. Calling it red-first would have been false; leaving it out would have left the new shape untested.
- **The second acceptance leg this session that asked for something the schema cannot express.** `.6.1.1` wanted a tenant-scoped policy bundle over a registry with no tenant column; this one wanted an action/scope check over vocabularies that cannot name a publication. Both legs were written from the defect's shape rather than from the model's, which is the right instinct producing an unmeetable requirement. Both are now routed to leaves that own the design question instead of being met in a weaker form.

## 2026-09-14 — The copy was the defect, not the three missing checks

- I went in expecting to add three authorization calls to three tool bodies. What was actually wrong is that `reasonbraid-mcp` had its own `pub mod server` — a 5,366-character re-implementation of reads the server already had — and the module header asserted the two were the same. Adding the checks beside the copy would have left the mechanism intact: a second implementation of an authorization is free to omit it again, and the sentence asserting sameness is what the next reader trusts instead of the query.
- So the repair is structural. Three halves came out of `api.rs` — `authorize_inspection`, `thread_inspection`, `inbox_inspection` — and both surfaces call them. The copy is gone. I can no longer write a true-sounding sentence about sameness that the compiler would not check.
- **The thing I got wrong, and caught by measuring rather than by reading.** The finding I inherited said the policy bundle returns "every tenant's policy documents". I set out to add a tenant filter and went looking for the column. There is no column. `git grep -n "tenant"` over the migration and the whole `policy.rs` returns rc=1 — the word does not appear. The registry is site-wide, the HTTP surface returns the same set to any enrolled caller, and I had been about to "fix" a design by adding a `WHERE` to one of six sites while the write surface admits any enrolled principal. The oracle was sitting in the finding itself: the handler the module claimed parity with.
- What that changed: acceptance leg 1 asked me to seed a policy in tenant B. There is no such row to seed. I rewrote the leg before meeting it and said so in the leaf, rather than meeting a weaker version quietly. The real debts were the enrolment gate the tool never ran and the `{"tenant_id": …}` label that told the caller these were their policies.
- ⭐ **I made the control observable on the point I was correcting.** Leg D now has Bob register a policy and asserts Alice — in the other tenant — sees it. The site-global property is now a thing the suite demonstrates, so `.6.1.5` inherits a demonstration instead of my description of one.
- **A fail-fast control was the wrong shape for this claim and the first run proved it.** The claim is "all three read tools". The control stopped at the first breach and told me about one tool. Accumulating the legs and asserting once at the end turned one red leg into eight, which is the picture I actually needed.
- ⛔ **`git diff` cannot prove a neutralization landed in a NEW file.** My falsification harness printed the diff as its evidence that the gate had been removed. `mcp_read.rs` is new and untracked, so the diff was empty — and the harness happily reported success. The run's own result caught it. Replaced with a grep census of the gate calls, which does not care about a file's tracking state.
- **The repaired malformed-principal case failed my own control by refusing correctly.** The tool refuses an unparseable principal through `Err(ErrorData)` at the protocol level — which is where the write tools already put one — and my control asserted on `CallToolResult::error`. I had keyed the test on the transport instead of the property. The leg now asserts that no bundle came back, either way.

## 2026-09-14 — An underscore was the whole finding

- `server::policy_bundle(pool, _tenant_id)`. The underscore is the compiler being told the argument is unused, and eleven lines above it the module says every read runs the reader classification. The query is `SELECT policy_id, version, digest, clauses FROM policy_versions ORDER BY policy_id, version`. No `WHERE`. Every tenant's policy clauses, to any caller, wrapped in a JSON object whose `tenant_id` field is the caller's own. ⛔ **CORRECTED by `.6.1.1` (REPAIR-0182):** `policy_versions` has NO tenant column — `git grep -n "tenant" -- migrations/0038_policy_registry.sql crates/reasonbraid-server/src/policy.rs` returns rc=1 — so the registry is SITE-GLOBAL and HTTP `api.rs::list_policies` returns the same unfiltered set to any enrolled caller. The missing `WHERE`, the unchecked principal and the false tenant label all STAND; the cross-tenant FRAMING does not. The schema question is `.6.1.5`.
- The record said "policy_bundle ignores tenant" and I nearly took that as the finding. It is smaller than what is there: the tool also ignores its `principal`, and the module's opening paragraph asserts the opposite of both. A claim in a doc comment is not decoration — it is what the next reader will rely on instead of reading the query.
- **`get_thread` is the interesting one because it does the work and throws it away.** It calls `classify`, which correctly returns `Network` for a principal in another tenant, and then returns the full projection regardless. The class is printed in the response as `"visibility": "network"`, beside the state it was supposed to restrict. A reader of the response would take that field as evidence the restriction happened.
- **Two of my measurements made the record's claim smaller, and I wrote them down as narrowings rather than quietly using the bigger version.** The `body["tenant_id"]` panic is real, but the MCP tool passes a typed payload, so it is not reachable that way today — which changes where the guard belongs, not whether one is needed. And the "consumes quota on every retry" clause turns out to be documented verbatim in the module header, so the leaf's job is to keep that sentence true, not to fix a contradiction.
- **The `none` row finally has an instance.** Six states were defined in tranche 1; five acquired instances over four tranches; `none` waited until a reviewer's page-boundary continuation marker showed up. I recorded it rather than skipping the clause, because a missing row and an overlooked clause look identical from outside.
- **`MEMORY.md` is at 7,161 of 7,168 bytes and I have now had to evict something three commits running.** I have also sharpened the shed rule twice in those three commits to keep the evictions honest. That pattern is the tell: when a selection rule needs re-deriving every time it is applied, the population wants partitioning, not a better rule. Routed to `.11.4.2` with the number, because raising the cap is the one repair the architecture document forbids by name.

## 2026-09-14 — The gate I built yesterday blocked me today

- `ATTACH-LANDED` went in one commit ago. The next tranche classified three clauses `attach`, I wrote the ledger rows, ran `--classified`, and it refused: three breaches, each naming the record, the owning leaf and what would happen if I shipped it. I had, in fact, not written the sentences yet. I would like to claim I was about to.
- That is the whole argument for mechanizing a rule instead of remembering it, and it arrived faster than I expected — one commit. The rule had been written down in the ledger's vocabulary for four commits and was violated once in that span.
- **Three sites, one predicate.** `event_id_for_operation` selects on `operation_id` alone. The receipt insert conflicts on `event_id`, which is the whole primary key. Migration 0021's view joins `e.operation_id = i.command_id`. Two of those came from one record and the third from a record five files away, and nothing in the tree had put them side by side — because the tree indexes by leaf, and the records index by reviewer. That is the gap this whole reconciliation activity exists to close, and this is the clearest instance it has produced.
- **The one I nearly got wrong was the date.** A test hardcoding `2026-09-15` read, on 2026-09-14, like a bomb with ten hours on it. The honest move was to read both sides before saying so: the writer never compares the expiry to `now()`, and the scheduler selects waivers with no expiry predicate at all. Nothing breaks tomorrow. Had I published "these tests fail tomorrow" I would have been wrong in a way that costs someone a night.
- **And the measurement that settled it found the real defect.** The absent expiry predicate means `repeated_waiver` fires on a waiver that lapsed a year ago, for ever — and on the FIRST waiver, since one row makes one pair. The record named the single-waiver half and not the expiry half. Answering a question with a question mark on it is usually worth more than confirming one without.
- **At the byte cap, the cheapest warning to shed turned out to be the one a gate now enforces.** `MEMORY.md` carries the rule "shed one whose line names its leaf", which left ten candidates. The sharper cut is: the machine says it better than the prose can, at the moment it matters, with the row named. I cut it to a pointer at the gate rather than deleting it, so a reader still knows the gate is there.

## 2026-09-14 — I had already written down what the gate would find

- `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.11` was opened with a census in it: *3 of 3 breaching at tranche 1's close, 0 of 15 today.* That number was obtained by hand, by reading leaves. The acceptance I wrote for myself said to reproduce it before registering anything, and the temptation was to treat "I already counted it" as reproduction.
- It is not. A hand count and an instrument can agree by accident, and the instrument is what ships. So: a detached worktree at each of the four tranche-closing commits, the new refusal copied in, run against that commit's own ledger and that commit's own trees. 3 / 0 / 0 / 0. Then the same four measured a second time by a throwaway re-implementation over `git show`, written before I touched the real script. Same answer.
- **The part that would have embarrassed me is the part the second route protects.** If the instrument had a bug that made it pass everything, the live ledger would still read clean and I would have registered a gate that gates nothing. The only defence is a point in history where the answer is known to be non-zero — and the reason one exists is that tranche 1 actually failed, and was written down instead of quietly fixed.
- **Boundary matching turned out to matter.** `R-53-4` is a prefix of `R-53-41`. A substring test would let a leaf that attached the longer record satisfy a row about the shorter one, forever, invisibly. One lookahead, one control, and the control is the half that will still be true in six months.
- I made the `sections` argument optional, and skipping the rule when it is absent felt like a hole until I wrote the control that names it: "no sections supplied" is a different claim from "the owner's section is empty". The synthetic parser controls legitimately have no sections. An owner with no section, *supplied*, is a breach — that is the case where nothing was written.
- **Two gates, same rule shape, opposite verdicts, and both decided by counting.** `.11.9` proposed a citation gate that would have fired on 114 of 131 and was rejected, because a gate people waive is a gate that lies. This one fires on 0 of 26 and would have caught all 3 historical instances. The house style is not "add a gate" or "don't add a gate" — it is "measure what it would fire on", and it keeps producing different answers.

## 2026-09-14 — The gate was wrong about the format it gates

- The record said "Table-arity parser treats pipes inside code as nonseparators, but GFM tables split unescaped pipes even code spans (must verify primary GFM spec when repairing), current selftest may encode falsegreen." Three claims, and the instinct is to go read the CommonMark and GFM specifications and reason about them.
- That instinct produces two parsers and no oracle. The project already holds the better move for code — *ask the library, never restate it* — and a document format is the same problem: the authority is the tool that renders the page, not the prose describing the format. So I built a two-page mdbook, put the disputed row in it, and read the HTML. **Two `<td>` cells where the header has two columns and the row has three parts; the third silently gone.** Seconds, not an argument.
- **Then the census, because one example is not a population.** Re-scanning all 322 tracked markdown files under both rules: the shipped gate finds 0, the renderer's rule finds 2. Rendering both confirmed them. One is `DOCTRINE_ENFORCEMENT.md:39`, whose third cell comes out as a bare `—` — the doctrine registry does not name the script that enforces `INDEX-FRONTIER` on its published page. The other was in `RECONCILIATION.md`, which this activity writes, truncating an Evidence cell mid-call.
- **I left the first one broken on purpose, and that is the part I want to argue for.** `TOOLBOX.md` already records that a self-test written alongside the code shares its blind spots — measured once on the route census, and now a second time on this parser, whose self-test asserts the wrong answer for the exact case.
