A repair verified only against fixtures the repairer invents is the same failure again. `DOCTRINE_ENFORCEMENT.md:39` is now the only real-world instance in the corpus, and `.11.2` should have to satisfy it.
- **The measurement that pleased me least was the one that refuted a remedy I had already half-written.** `R-90-1` says the demo's negative control lacks `pipefail`. It does. Adding `pipefail` changes nothing: `!` negates the pipeline's status, and that status is 1 whether the CLI died or merely found no match. I only learned this because I ran the four-way table instead of the two cases I expected to differ. A diagnosis can be right and its cure wrong, and the cure is the part that ships.
- **`--commands 0` prints PASS.** The load harness's exit gates are `[ TOTAL -ge COMMANDS ]` and `[ FAILED -eq 0 ]`, and zero satisfies both after issuing nothing. So does `-5`. A capacity number is the kind of claim `docs/CLAIM_VERIFICATION.md` exists for, and the harness that produces it can currently be green having measured nothing.
- **11 `attach` of 38 was the shape of the tranche, and it is not a comment on the earlier authors.** `.11.2` and `.11.3` write their goal lines as short lists of named mechanisms; `.6.2`, `.9.2`, `.7.1` write theirs as properties. A property generalises to a finding nobody anticipated; a list cannot. That is a fact about wording, and it predicts where the next tranches' `attach` rows will be.
- One premise came back false: `R-87-1` says this project retains `MAINTAINING.md`. It does not, and did not at the baseline — one `git ls-tree`. The sentinel finding underneath it is still real for any copy that does retain the file. Recording the refutation costs a sentence; leaving it implied would have let a future reader inherit the wrong blast radius.

## 2026-09-14 — The one that failed was the one I was proudest of

- Asked whether I trusted the session's findings, my honest reaction was to want to check about five things. That reaction is the verdict: the standard says the pass condition is answering in one word, and wanting the keyboard means the claims shipped before they were earned.
- ⭐ **The split was almost exactly the one `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.2.2.1` measured a day earlier, which is itself the finding.** Everything that came from a command held: the ratios, the counts, the 15 attach rows, the test totals. What failed came from a *pointer I hand-carried* — line numbers into a file I then spent four commits growing. I even published the six-row range and the two singletons in the same breath, and the six were durable for an accidental reason (nothing was inserted above line 92) while the two were dead within the hour.
- ⛔ The lesson is not "check line numbers". It is that **a reference's durability is a property of what it points at, not of how carefully it was taken.** `:3747` was correct when measured and re-measuring it would have confirmed it. The third leg — make it durable — is a different question from the first two, and a heading survives what a line number does not because it moves with the content it names. The project already has a gate for the dated version of this (`LIVE-DOC-CURRENCY`) and I walked into the undated version of the same thing.
- ⚠️ The softer one is worth more than it looks: the reproduction I published now returns 13 rather than 8, because commit-acceptance blocks are not leaf sections either and my own five added to the count. The number was never wrong — it was **unstable in a direction I did not name**, so a reader re-deriving it would find a discrepancy and have no way to tell whether they or I had erred. A measurement that grows when you write about it needs that said out loud.
- ⭐ The check I built to grade myself is the one the ledger has been missing all along: for every `attach` row, does the owning leaf's own section name its record? It ran 15 of 15 clean, and would have returned 3 of 3 breaching at tranche 1. So the gate `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.9` had to reject at 114-of-131 exists here at 0-of-15 — same instrument family, opposite verdict, because the population is bounded and the historical instances are real. Opened as `.11.11` rather than left in a reply, because a gate candidate described in a message is a suggestion.
- On the ones that held: I want to record that `git.effective` was worth re-checking by grep rather than by re-reading. Reading the function again would have repeated the blind spot of reading it the first time; grepping the field name asked a different question — *does this identifier appear anywhere in the file* — and answered it with comments and a declaration and nothing else. Dimensionally different, which is the whole point of the standard.

## 2026-09-14 — The consequence the inference did not reach

- When I opened this leaf I wrote down what I expected the blast radius to be, and marked it explicitly as an inference: no `panic = "abort"` in the workspace, no catch-panic layer in the server, therefore the request's task unwinds, the connection drops, the transaction rolls back, the process lives. Then I measured it, and all four were right.
- ⭐ **And the measurement still found something the inference had not, which is the whole argument for measuring anyway.** The probe printed `token used_at: Some(None)`. The token is left unconsumed — which sounds harmless until you remember that an outstanding unused token is exactly what refuses a second issuance for the same node id. A single mistyped host claim therefore takes that node id out of service for the token's lifetime. I had reasoned correctly about everything the panic *destroyed* and not at all about what it *left behind*.
- ⚠️ It is bounded to an hour by default because `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.4.1.1` already repaired lapsed-token supersession. Before that leaf, the same typo would have closed the node id permanently. Two defects that are each survivable alone compose into one that is not, and neither leaf could have seen it from its own side.
- On where to put the check: the fallible signature alone would have been a complete repair of the *clause* and a poor repair of the *problem*. It turns the panic into a 400 for the node — but the node is not who made the mistake, and the node id is still spent until the token lapses. Moving the refusal to issuance costs an operator one corrected field and deletes the bad state entirely. The clause was about the `expect`; the defect was about where the error surfaces.
- ⛔ I deliberately did not narrow the accept set, and this was the decision I thought hardest about. rcgen accepts an empty string, three hundred characters, `*` and `..` as a subject alternative name, which is plainly too much for something a verifier reads as a host identity. But narrowing is a compatibility change against deployments I cannot see, and `1.2.3.4` is a perfectly plausible thing an operator has already enrolled. Asking the library its own verdict moves the refusal earlier while changing nothing about *which* claims work — a repair that is provably free of that risk — and the grammar question gets its own record with the cost stated rather than being smuggled into a bug fix.
- ⚠️ A small thing worth keeping: `expect_err` would not compile, because `IssuedLeaf` carries `key_der` and derives no `Debug`. The five-second fix is to add the derive. That would put a private key one formatting call away from any panic message or log line, to save a test one method call. `.err().expect(...)` needs no `Debug` at all. Absences like that are load-bearing, and a test is exactly the place where someone removes one without noticing.

## 2026-09-14 — Ask the library, not the call site

- `CertificateParams::new(vec![host_claim]).expect("leaf params")` is the kind of line you can stare at and still not know whether it is a bug. The panic is obviously *possible*; whether it is *reachable* depends entirely on what rcgen refuses, and nothing at the call site tells you. I wrote an eight-input probe. It took two minutes and the answer was not the one I would have written down from the code: rcgen 0.14.10 accepted the empty string, three hundred characters, a wildcard, `..` and a claim with spaces and exclamation marks, and refused exactly one — the non-ASCII one.
- ⭐ **That single measurement flipped the shape of the finding twice.** First it narrowed the defect the record named: "malformed host" sounds like a wide class and is a very small one. Then it opened a bigger one the record was not pointing at — if almost everything is accepted as a DNS SAN, the certificate's host binding is not validating anything, and a SAN is the host identity a verifier reads. The clause I was classifying turned out to be the smaller half of what the probe found.
- ⚠️ I wrote "eight inputs are a SAMPLE, not the boundary" into the new leaf in those words, because the tempting next sentence — "only non-ASCII panics" — is exactly the kind of claim this repository has been burned by: a number or a rule derived from the search I happened to run. I do not know rcgen's accept set. I know eight points on it.
- ⚠️ The same restraint applies to the blast radius. I measured two things: the workspace declares no `[profile]`, and the server installs no `CatchPanicLayer`. From those the expectation is that the request's task unwinds, the connection drops and the transaction rolls back, with the process surviving. That is an inference, and `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.4.2.7`'s panic cost a whole node process — so the leaf says reproduce it before claiming either size. Assuming this one is smaller *because it looks smaller* is the error that leaf exists to warn about.
- ⭐ On the first `declined` row: it was more uncomfortable to write than any of the defects. Declining is the only state that removes a clause from view, and there is no instrument that can check it — the whole reason the state was written into the vocabulary before an instance existed. What made it defensible was separating the two halves: the mechanism the clause describes is real and I recorded it, and only the *contradiction* it asserts fails, because the documented promise it names is about hidden fields and an unset visible field is a different thing. I kept the owner in the row so a reader can reopen it against a claim I did not check.
- The eight `attach` rows had one shape in common that is worth naming: every one lived in the surface its leaf owns but outside the part the goal line points at. "Prevent private-feature leaks" sends a reader to the visibility *enforcement*, and all three profile clauses are in the default, the doc comment and the field list. That is not carelessness in the goal lines — a goal line is a summary — it is just what a summary does, which is `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.9`'s finding restated from the other end.

## 2026-09-14 — Two failure families, one return type

- `regions::route` made three database reads and wrapped each in `.map_err(|_| RegionRefusal::…)`. The shape is so ordinary that it reads as tidy error handling, and what it actually does is convert "I could not find out" into "I found out, and the answer is no". Those are different facts, and only one of them is about the site.
- The reproduction is the part I want to keep. Closing the pool is a deterministic way to make every read fail without touching a row, so the probe could **assert its own premise first** — `dev-local` is declared, proved by a query against the live pool — and only then break the storage. The output is then unarguable: `UndeclaredRegion { region: "dev-local" }` for a region the same test just read out of the table.
- ⭐ The fix was not a discovery. `site_authority/registry.rs` already carries the rule in a comment, written by the leaf that repaired the *other* verb the same review record named: "an unavailable database must never masquerade as an undeclared region." One record, two verbs, one repair. That is `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.9`'s mechanism in its plainest form, and the interesting thing is that the leaf which fixed `pair` had already written down exactly why `route` was wrong.
- ⚠️ On falsifying a type change: you cannot revert the file, because the new control will not compile against the old signature, and a control that cannot run is not a control that went red. The honest neutralization keeps the new type and restores only the superseded *classification* — the `map_err` — which is the thing the repair actually changed. It gave `2 passed; 1 failed` with the failure naming the old answer verbatim, and the two untouched tests stayed green, which is the evidence that the injection was the defect and not something adjacent to it.
- ⭐ Ordering inside the control matters more than it looks. The positive arm (a genuinely undeclared region is still refused) runs **first, on a live pool**. Put it after `pool.close()` and it cannot run at all; leave it out and a repair that simply stopped refusing anything would pass. The negative arm then also asserts the *message*, not just the variant — a caller that logs the `Display` and nothing else must not still be told the declaration is missing.
- ⛔ I deliberately left two things alone that a wider reading would have swept up: the `Option<bool>` + `unwrap_or(false)` decode (`SELECT EXISTS` never returns NULL, so it is dead defensiveness — but it is a different clause) and the two near-identical declaration blocks that a loop would collapse. The leaf said the clause is the error classification of three reads, and a diff that is exactly that is a diff a reviewer can check against the claim.

## 2026-09-14 — A state whose next action is not "none"

- The reconciliation ledger has six states. Five of them end the matter: `handled`, `owned`, `declined` and `none` need nothing further, and `unowned` is loud because it demands a new leaf. `attach` is the odd one — it means *a leaf owns this surface, its own text does not mention this clause, and its next census will therefore drop it* — and its next action is a sentence that has to be written into somebody else's section.
- Tranche 1 recorded three `attach` rows and wrote none of the three sentences. I found it by reading `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.7.1` and `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.4` whole before classifying my own, which is the only way to find it: the rows were correct, the instrument was clean, and `--classified` had nothing to complain about. **A row describing a future failure does not prevent it. Only the sentence in the other leaf does.**
- ⭐ The general shape is worth carrying: **when a classification has a side effect on a document the classifier does not own, the classification is not finished when the row is written.** Everything about `attach` was right except the part that happened somewhere else, and nothing in the tooling could tell. The fix is a commit boundary in the vocabulary rather than a checker, because a checker would have to decide whether a paraphrase counts, which is `SIGNOFF-REPAIR.11.6`'s measured failure mode.
- On the split: I sized tranche 2 against tranche 1 before deciding, and the ratio (2.8x by character) is what made the decision instead of a feeling about how long it would take. The boundary is then the ranking's own — each record's narrowest candidate leaf — so the three children each cover one surface and a reader of one child reads one module's source. A split chosen for size alone would have mixed `ca.rs`, `publisher.rs` and the profile visibility ladder into one commit for no reason but arithmetic.
- On the population correction: eight uncited records are named in the tree, in text belonging to no leaf. ⭐ A second number moved while I was writing, and it is the good kind: the seven `attach` sentences made `.7.1`, `.8.1` and `.11.4` genuinely NAME six records, so the uncited count fell 112 → 106 in this same commit. The instrument's own output predicts exactly that convergence, and watching it happen is a cheaper proof that the attachments landed than any assertion I could write about them. Six sit in `#### Historical census dispositions for .2.2`, a heading shape the instrument does not recognise as a leaf — so its rows bound the previous leaf's section and fall outside every one. ⚠️ **The instrument is not wrong and I made a point of not saying it was.** Its rule is "a leaf's own section" and a table under a later non-leaf heading genuinely is not that leaf speaking. What was wrong was the sentence a reader could build from the number. The distinction matters because the alternative — quietly "fixing" the instrument to attribute stray text to the nearest leaf above — would make every commit-acceptance block count as a citation and destroy the measure.
- Four live defects came out of the six records, and all four were already named in their owning leaf's goal line, which is a good result rather than a disappointing one: it means the classification found real work with a home, and the correct action was to record the row and not invent a leaf. The two I did not expect were the reconciler reading `git.effective` in no branch at all — the field is carried, stored and never consulted, and a unit test asserts the resulting blindness as correct behaviour — and `POST /v1/workflow-profiles` admitting on enrolment alone, which is weaker than the endpoint next to it and lets any enrolled principal shadow a built-in deliberation profile for the whole site.
- ⚠️ The egress clause is recorded as `attach` rather than a defect on purpose. `resolvers::resolve` applies `declared >= required` to two ladders; for sandbox levels "greater" means more isolation and the comparison refuses a downgrade, for egress classes "greater" means more network reach and ADR-018 says in its own words that the egress claim is "the MAXIMUM, never the minimum". Comparing ceilings with `>=` admits a broader ceiling than asked. The module's comment reads the field the other way. Two tracked documents, two readings, no control that distinguishes them — and deciding which one binds is ADR-018's revisit trigger, not something to settle inside a reconciliation leaf.

## 2026-09-14 — The one-word test, and what failed it

- Being asked "do you still stand by it?" is defined in this repository's claim standard as a grading event, and the pass condition is answering **yes immediately, with no keyboard**. My honest reaction was to want to check three things, which is itself the verdict: a re-audit triggered by the question is evidence the claim shipped before it was earned.
- ⭐ So I checked, and the split is clean enough to be worth a rule. **Everything that came from a command held. Everything that came from my memory of a source did not.** The seven sites, the two device ids, the 265 files, the 22 classifications, the 36-against-46 — all reproduce exactly. The three corrections are a verb (*clone* where the script says `git init`), a relationship (*its header cites that incident* where the header names only its own flag), and an attribution (*the record was pointing at that directory* where the record names roles, not paths).
- ⚠️ None of the three changed a number or a decision, and that is exactly why they were easy to write and would have been easy to leave. A named instance has no tolerance band. The verb correction is also strictly more informative than the error was: the script that really clones is a third one I had not read, and it was inside the seven the whole time.
- 🔴 The one I most want to remember is the fourth, because it never reached a file. I told the director that "a description of a check was ahead of the check" **three times today** and offered it as the largest unguarded failure mode in the spine. Two of those are the same pattern; the third is an instrument matching a literal in its own source, which is a different family with its own founding incident. ⭐ **Merging two patterns inflates the evidence for both.** Two instances in one day is still worth raising; three was a number I assembled rather than counted, in the same message where I was warning about exactly that.
- ⚠️ The uncomfortable part is the timing. Every one of these was written in a *summary*, at the end, away from the source — the same motion that produced the stale frontier caption and the test comment claiming coverage it never had. The defect is not carelessness in reading; it is confidence in recall while writing the part other people will quote.

## 2026-09-13 — I published a five and the tree held a seven, and the filter that did it was mine

- The record said "tracked **task-acceptance and waiver-routing probe scripts** both `WORK=$(mktemp -d)`". I read "probe scripts" as the guards, censused `scripts/`, found five ambient sites, repaired them, and published five. The two files whose variable is literally named `WORK` are `docs/tasks/artifacts/task_acceptance/run_task_acceptance_probes.sh` and its waiver-routing twin — and my census command ended in `| grep -v '^docs/'`. ⭐ **I filtered out the directory the record was pointing at.**
- ⚠️ The guards were not a wrong find. The record's own last clause predicted them — "guards themselves may mktemp similarly" — and they were breaching. What I got wrong is the *number*, and a number is what a later reader quotes. Seven, not five, and the superseded five stays in the leaf above the correction rather than being edited into agreement.
- 🔴 What makes this worth writing down rather than just fixing: `MEMORY.md` has carried the warning for days. *"Scope a census to `crates/` WHOLE — `git grep -- 'crates/*/src'` silently matches NOTHING."* Same defect class, different mechanism — a glob that matches nothing and a filter that excludes a directory both hand you a smaller world with no error to notice. Knowing the rule did not stop me; what stopped me was running the census a second time, unfiltered, for a different purpose (deciding the gate's population). ⭐ **The re-run that catches you is usually the one you did for another reason.**
- ⭐ The gate decision turned on a distinction I did not expect to need. The obvious pattern is "flag `mktemp` / flag `tempfile`". That would have condemned fifteen conforming sites, including every one I had just repaired — because `mktemp` is the *right* tool here. It names by exclusive creation, which is exactly the half of this doctrine a clock-derived name gets wrong. **The breach is the argument, not the call**: a missing repository-derived template, a missing `dir=`. A gate that flags the call teaches people to hand-roll something worse.
- ⚠️ I gave shell and Python a comment skip that Rust does not get, and I want the asymmetry on the record as a judgement rather than a convenience. Every site I repaired carries a comment *explaining the rule and naming the command* — a comment-sensitive shell rule would flag its own documentation for ever. In Rust, `env::temp_dir()` inside a comment is usually commented-out code, and the arm asserting that predates me. Both directions are now asserted in the self-test, so the next person sees it was decided.
- 🔴 And then the gate flagged itself. Its diagnostic message spelled the command it matches, so the message was a breach — the self-test doctrine's founding incident, happening again, in a file whose own header cites that incident. I re-worded the message instead of excluding the file's path, because an exclusion list has to stay right and a re-wording polices itself: spell the literal again and the whole-tree scan goes red on this file immediately. The self-test fixtures assemble the command from two halves for the same reason.

## 2026-09-13 — A census can be right about its own scope and silent about yours

- The inbox had four administrative verbs and a repair a few commits ago bound three of them to their tenant. That repair's census opened by measuring "four of the five **mutations** carry no tenant predicate" — which is true, and careful, and the word doing the damage is *mutations*. The record it was working from named four verbs including the read. A census answers the question it was given; it does not tell you that the question was narrower than the record that prompted it.
- ⭐ That is the same failure as the one the clause-ledger exists for, one level down. A split reads the leaf's goal line instead of the routed record; here a census read its own scope line instead of the routed record. Both are rigorous work on a slightly wrong list, and rigour is exactly what makes them invisible — a careful census of the wrong set looks identical to a careful census.
- ⭐ The general form I got out of it is sharper than "check for a tenant predicate", and I only got it because one of the three census hits was a false positive. `inspect_call` looks guilty by grep — its queries are `WHERE call_id = $1` with no tenant anywhere — and it is correct, because it loads the call *first* and authorises against `call.tenant_id`. The tenant is **derived from the target**. The broken handler accepted the tenant *and* the node from the caller and checked one of them. ⭐ **Two independent identifiers from the caller must be bound together; one derived from the other needs no binding at all.** A grep for the column would have flagged the correct handler and could still have missed a handler that names the column in the wrong statement.
- ⚠️ I wrote the census's limit into the leaf rather than letting the number stand on its own: 10 of the 24 handlers have no SQL in the handler body, so the scan never followed them. They are *unmeasured*, not clean, and there is now a leaf that owns following each one into its delegate. A census that publishes 14 measured routes as if it had measured 24 is the kind of number that gets quoted later.
- 🔴 The part that stings: the control I most needed already existed in *prose*. The sibling test opens "an administrator of one tenant must not be able to **read**, move or destroy another tenant's inbox — and before this repair it could do all three." It asserts two. And that sentence had been copied into two book chapters, one of which says "all three verbs are bound to your tenant" immediately under a code block showing four. So the read was described as covered in three places and covered in none. ⭐ **A description of a check is not its coverage**, and unlike a stale number there is no gate that can notice — a comment has no output to compare against.
- ⚠️ On falsification I deliberately did *not* claim more than I did. The control was written before the fix, so the pre-repair run is the neutralised build; I did not revert anything afterwards to produce a second red run, and the leaf says that rather than implying a separate experiment. It is actually the stronger evidence — nothing was reconstructed, so the hazard of neutralising a *different* mechanism (which this tree was burned by at 4193 against 242) cannot arise.

## 2026-09-13 — The ranking I was told to use was backwards, and the record that proves it says nothing at all

- I inherited an instruction from the leaf that opened this one: rank the backlog by fan-out, small first, because a record naming one leaf was making a real assignment. It reads right. I measured it anyway, because this repository's own doctrine says to count the population before proposing a rule over it — and the measurement inverted the order. ⭐ **A fan-out of one is not precision; it is the reviewer reaching for the default bucket.** Every one of the five single-candidate records points at a container leaf, and one container is named by 99 of the 131 records. Meanwhile a record naming eight leaves is the one that reached `.6.3`, a leaf exactly one record in the entire corpus names — and when `.6.3` is eventually censused, that record is the only one it has.
- ⭐ `R-6-27-1` is the instance I keep. It has a fan-out of one. Its body is the artifact's boilerplate sentence about the full-read prerequisite. It states **no finding whatsoever**, and it still has a candidate leaf. Any ranking that puts it first is ranking on the wrong axis, and no amount of reading the prose would have told me — only counting who else names its target did.
- ⚠️ I was careful not to write "the earlier leaf was wrong", and that carefulness is the point rather than politeness. Its sentence is about the reviewer's confidence and is true on that. Mine is about whether the target leaf depended on the record. Two different questions, and a re-derivation that disagrees with a published number is the *newer* instrument, so it carries the heavier burden. Naming the difference cost one sentence and keeps both facts.
- ⭐ The classification vocabulary grew a state while I was using it. The acceptance said "handled / declined / unowned". Working through the first seven records, the state that kept appearing was none of those: a leaf *owns* the surface, but the leaf's own text does not mention the clause — so when that leaf is eventually censused and split it will read its goal line and drop the clause, which is the exact defect this whole family exists to fix. I called it `attach`, because the fix is one sentence in the owning leaf and it is only cheap *now*. Three clauses carry it. A vocabulary that could not express it would have recorded them as "owned" and lost them.
- ⚠️ `declined` has zero rows, and I wrote it into the closed set anyway with a note saying so. It is the one state no search can ever find, so if it is not in the vocabulary before the first instance, the first instance gets recorded as something else.
- 🔴 My own prose broke the instrument, which is the most useful thing that happened. I wrote two new leaves that cite a census record and then cite source lines — `api.rs:1467`, five `scripts/check_*.sh:NN`. The instrument's elided-citation rule ("a bare `:N` inherits the nearest census filename") swallowed all eight, four of them landing inside real records, and the headline population moved from 114 to 111 without a word. ⭐ I only saw it because I re-ran the census after the edit and the number had changed by three when I had cited two records. **Re-run an instrument after editing the corpus it reads, and look at whether the number moved by the amount you caused.**
- ⚠️ And the same class of error was sitting in the frontier caption the whole time. It published a pending-leaf count with the command that derives it — which is the right instinct — but the command matched `- Status: pending` and the convention had since introduced `- Opened: pending` for a leaf that has never closed. Eleven leaves were invisible to it. A derived number is only as good as the derivation, and a derivation ages exactly like a hand-typed constant when the thing it reads changes shape.

## 2026-09-13 — The repair that a later legitimate operation undid

- Three clauses of one census record, and I repaired them one at a time so I would see each fail on its own. That cost three extra suite runs and was worth every one of them: "confirmed at the source" is a reading, and `heartbeat after the replacement: 200` is a fact.
- 🔴 The one I did not expect is the third. A few days ago a leaf stopped a revoked node renewing its lease by folding *does this node hold a usable certificate?* into the renewal's `WHERE`. That repair is correct. What I had not noticed is that the predicate quantifies over the node **id**, and a replacement enrollment issues a brand-new certificate for that same id — so the guard becomes true again, and the machine that was replaced gets its session back. ⭐ **A repair's predicate can be re-satisfied by a later, entirely legitimate operation.** That is not a bug in the earlier repair and it is not a bug in the replacement; it is what happens when a guard is written about an identity rather than about an event.
- ⚠️ I nearly deleted the lease row instead of bumping its epoch, which would have been neat and wrong. `issue_lease`'s INSERT path resets the epoch to 1, and one commit ago I argued — in writing, in a leaf — that `(node_id, lease_epoch)` determines the fencing token because epochs are monotonic per node. Deleting would have quietly invalidated my own reasoning from yesterday. Reading back what I had claimed before choosing the mechanism is the only reason I caught it.
- ⛔ The comment that hid the incarnation defect deserves its own note: *"Re-enrollment cannot duplicate: the `nodes` primary key refuses a second enroll for the id."* It is confidently wrong, it is adjacent to the code it is wrong about, and a replacement is exactly the case it fails to consider — the branch updates rather than inserts, so the primary key never sees it. I corrected it in place rather than just adding the fix, because the next reader would have trusted it the same way I nearly did.
- ⭐ And I measured the incarnation consequence rather than inheriting it. The record implies multiple current rows break selection. They do not: both selectors order by `valid_from DESC LIMIT 1` and were already picking the newest. The real damage is to the ledger — two rows each claiming to be current, so nothing can be attributed to one identity. Narrower, still real, and the leaf says which.

## 2026-09-13 — Four ways to say yes

- The record said the wake gate is only tested at concurrency zero and two. Enumerating the filter's states rather than the tests' gave five, and the interesting part is that three of the five uncovered ones all answer "deliver" — which makes them look interchangeable and they are not. A missing `concurrency` key delivers because `NULL = 0` is NULL; a missing `availability` block delivers because the whole JSON path collapses to NULL; a negative number delivers because it is a number that is not zero. Three different SQL mechanisms, one observable outcome. A single control would have covered one of them and looked like it covered all three.
- ⭐ The arm I nearly left out is the one I would keep if I could only keep one: re-asserting the DRAIN state at the end, after the four delivering states. Four assertions that a row arrives are all satisfied by a fixture that has quietly become deliverable for some reason having nothing to do with the gate. Putting zero back last means the whole block is measuring the gate rather than the fixture's mood.
- 🔴 I also went hunting for a defect that is not there, and I am recording it because the hypothesis was good. `(profile->'availability'->>'concurrency')::bigint` appears in six query sites, and a non-numeric JSON value raises at every one — an unvalidated field reaching a cast, which is exactly the shape of a real defect repaired here a few days ago. It is unreachable: the field is typed `Option<i64>` and the writer stores `serde_json::to_value(profile)`, the re-serialized struct rather than the raw body. ⭐ The type system is the validation, and it holds *because* the write path round-trips through it — which is a property worth knowing rather than assuming, since storing the raw body would have been the more obvious implementation.
- ⚠️ The substantive answer turned out to be a narrowing rather than a repair. The gate tests for exactly zero and never looks at an active count, so `concurrency: 2` does not cap anything. The record observed that correctly; it was never claimed. What was missing is that nobody had written down what the number *does* mean, so a reader would reasonably assume it limits. The book now says it is a switch with one meaningful value and that a real limit has to be enforced at the node.

## 2026-09-13 — The bound was the work

- The record said "rotate installs fresh identity only in memory, rb-node cert/key on disk not updated". Confirming that took two greps. Working out what it *costs* took the rest of the leaf, and it is the part I would have got wrong if I had written it up from the source reading.
- ⭐ My first instinct was "every restart after a rotation strands the node", which is false: the stale certificate loads, `cert_expires_soon()` is still true — it is *why* the node rotated — so the next handshake rotates again and the node heals itself. My second instinct was then "so it is cosmetic", which is also false. The node that stays down until that certificate actually expires cannot rotate out, because rotation requires a usable certificate, and it needs an operator to issue a fresh enrollment token. A window of at most five minutes on a ten-minute leaf, and on the wrong side of it the node is bricked.
- ⚠️ Both wrong instincts were confident and neither needed a test to produce. Writing the bound down carefully is what separated them, and it changed the leaf from "tidy this up" to "this is `.4.1.1`'s lockout through a different door".
- ⛔ The design decision I am most sure of is that the channel does not grow a filesystem. It has no file I/O anywhere, and it does not know where node-local state lives; `Node::open` does, because it is handed the journal path. So the channel gets a hook and the owner supplies the behaviour. ⭐ And the control asserts the two *specific filenames* rather than "a sink fired" — a sink writing to the wrong directory would satisfy the weaker assertion perfectly while leaving the defect exactly where it was.
- ⭐ Persist-first, then swap memory. I nearly wrote it the other way because installing first reads more naturally. A crash between the two steps decides it: new-on-disk/old-in-memory is corrected by the next start, old-on-disk/new-in-memory is precisely the bug I am fixing. The control observes memory from *inside* the sink, which is an odd-looking test and the only way to assert an ordering that has no other observable.
- ⚠️ clippy refused the commit twice for "very complex type". The lazy answer is an `#[allow]`; the right one is the type alias it was asking for, and naming `IdentitySink` gave me a place to say that a sink receives the certificate *and its key* — a certificate without its key restores nothing, which is worth stating once rather than hoping the next caller notices.

## 2026-09-13 — I falsified the wrong thing first, and the number told me

- The defect is small and the shape is worth keeping: two `Arc<Mutex<_>>` fields holding one fact. The WRITE took both locks together, so two handshakes could never interleave, and that is exactly why it reads as correct. Every READ took them separately. A reader does not need a torn write to see a torn pair — it only needs a complete write to land between its two acquisitions.
- ⭐ The control I am happy with is the self-describing generation: generation `n` is token `fnc_n` with epoch `n`, so any pair a reader sees can be checked against itself. No bookkeeping about which generation "should" be live, no sampling, no timing assumption — the invariant is local to the observation.
- 🔴 **And then I falsified the wrong mechanism.** My first neutralization split the WRITE into two acquisitions. The control went red immediately — `4193 torn of 40000` — and I nearly wrote that number down as the reproduction. It is not: the superseded code's write was atomic. What I had built was a different bug that happens to fail the same assertion. ⚠️ The tell was the magnitude. When I redid it faithfully, by splitting the READ, the rate dropped to `242 of 40000`. A falsification that makes the defect ten times louder than it was is not evidence about the repair, it is evidence that I broke something else.
- ⭐ That 0.6 % is also the answer to "why did nobody notice". A real node reads this pair a few times a second and handshakes on reconnect; the window is a few instructions wide. It would present as an occasional unexplained `401` followed by a successful reconnect — indistinguishable from a network blip, and therefore retried away forever.
- ⚠️ I wrote the bound into the leaf before doing the work: this is availability, not a fencing bypass, because the server refuses the mismatched pair. Stating that first kept me honest about the fix's justification — a request that cannot possibly succeed should not be constructible is a good reason; "a security race" would have been a false one.
- ⛔ And the LOCKSTEP line says "none needed" explicitly rather than being left blank. The change is internal to the node client and alters no wire field, no route and no documented behaviour, so the book stays as it is — but a blank lockstep is indistinguishable from a forgotten one.

## 2026-09-13 — The leaf that avoided the defect committed it anyway

- `.4.2` made a point, at its split, of reading the fifteen source-census records routed to it rather than only its own goal line — explicitly citing `.11.9`'s lesson "applied the day it was written". I ran the instrument over it expecting to confirm that, and it did: fifteen records, fifteen cited, zero uncited, against `.4.1`'s thirty-six uncited. The claim was true.
- 🔴 Then I read the record bodies, which is a different activity from checking citations, and found **six clauses the split did not carry**. In the leaf that had just avoided the citation form of exactly this defect. ⭐ That is the strongest possible evidence for the reframing `.11.9` arrived at yesterday — the unit is a clause with an owner, not a record with a citation — and I would not have believed it as strongly from any argument. Reading every record you are sent and still dropping a sentence inside one is not carelessness; it is what happens when a record holds four findings and you are looking for the one you came for.
- ⚠️ Two of the six I could check in a minute and did: `current_fencing_token` and `current_lease_epoch` take two different mutexes, so any request reading both can straddle a handshake. It is an availability bug rather than a fencing bypass — the server refuses the mismatched pair — and I wrote that bound into the leaf *before* the work rather than leaving it to be discovered as a caveat afterwards. `install_identity` writes only an in-memory value, which makes "does a rotation survive a restart?" a question rather than an answer.
- ⛔ The other four I deliberately did NOT verify. They are recorded as the reviewer's claims with the reproduce-before-writing-up prohibition attached. There is a real temptation, when you have found six things, to write all six up as defects because six sounds better than two; the honest split is two measured and four claimed, and the leaves say which is which.
- ⭐ The grouping decision I am most pleased with is the replacement one. Three separate clauses — the stale `host_id`, the unclosed incarnation rows, the unfenced old lease — are all the same question: *what is a replacement supposed to end?* Opened separately they would get three answers that need not agree. Opened together, with a pointer to the earlier decision that deliberately preserves a revoked node's tail, the next person at least knows the two properties are in tension before choosing.
- ⚠️ The pending count went from 36 to 40 on a commit that repaired nothing. That is the right outcome and worth saying plainly: a reconciliation that finds work makes the tree bigger, and a tree that only ever shrinks is a tree that has stopped looking.

## 2026-09-13 — Two gates, one rule shape, opposite answers

- Yesterday's version of me would have added ten codes to the registry and called it a reconciliation. What stopped that was reading the module's own doc comment, which I had not done before opening the leaf: the registry says in as many words that it is the *complete* §9.8 list rather than a subset of what this build uses, and that codes beyond it are preserved verbatim by `ReasonCode::Unknown`. Both halves of the finding were already answered, in the file, by the person who wrote it.
- ⛔ And then the leaf's numbers were wrong in the way the leaf was about. It said 19 emitted, 10 unregistered. It is 18 and 9: `unknown` appears in the grep twice, both times in a CLIENT crate, building a local error when a response body will not parse. It never travels the wire. A raw search count published as a defect count — the exact anti-pattern this repository has a lesson about — inside a leaf whose subject is a published set nobody re-derived. I put the client/server split into the instrument's self-test in both directions, because it is the one thing that can silently inflate the number again.
- 🔴 What was actually broken was none of that: the book had no error page. A client author reads §9.8, sees twenty codes, and has no way to discover `quota_unconfigured` short of receiving one. The fix is a table, derived from the sources, saying which codes exist, what status each carries, which are §9.8-stable and which this build added.
- ⭐ **The part I find genuinely interesting is that I registered a gate here one commit after rejecting one.** The rule shape is identical — "a published set must cover what the code does" — and the measurement answered oppositely. The citation gate would have fired on 114 of 131 records on day one, which is a backlog wearing a gate's clothes. This one fires on zero and would have fired on all nine, which means it catches the *next* drift instead of presenting a debt people learn to waive. That is not a house style, it is a test, and it is worth saying out loud that the same proposal can be right and wrong depending only on what you counted.
- ⛔ I did not touch `ROADMAP.md`. §9.8 is in a document frozen at v0.4.1 to errata and security corrections, and the freeze record says a v0.5.0 must cite measurements produced by working code. Nine emitted codes measured against the published list is precisely that — which makes it evidence *for* that decision, not licence for a repair leaf to edit a frozen document on its own authority. Routed with the measurement attached.
- ⚠️ One more pathspec trap, second time today: `git grep -- 'crates/*/src'` silently returns nothing, because the glob matches the directory rather than files beneath it. A zero from a malformed search reads exactly like a real absence. I noticed only because I expected a non-zero answer; if I had expected zero I would have published it.

## 2026-09-13 — The test I did not write was the one that settled it

- This started as a documentation mismatch — two comments saying "an authorized human" over a route that never checked — and I expected to spend the leaf deciding which of the two was wrong. What settled it was not my reasoning and not my new control. It was the falsification: I added the human-kind check the old sentence implied, expecting my own control to go red, and **three** tests failed. Two of them I had never read.
- ⭐ Those two already drive an agent role at the issuance route and require it to be adjudicated by the GRANT — one asserting a `403` with a denial record, one asserting a token dies with the authority that issued it. So the project had asserted grant-based issuance for agents long before anyone asked the question in words. That is the "prefer an oracle you did not build" leg of `CLAIM_VERIFICATION.md` landing squarely, and it is worth more than my four lines of roadmap citation.
- 🔴 And one of them fails for a reason I would not have predicted: a kind check refuses with `401` *before* the authorization step, so the denial record never gets written. The check would not merely have restricted agents — it would have silently removed the audit evidence the denial path exists to produce. A guard placed in front of the authorization is a guard that erases the reason.
- ⚠️ The census widened the leaf, again. I was opened on two doc sites; grepping the sentence found six, including `POST /v1/nodes/revoke` carrying exactly the same claim over exactly the same grant. If I had fixed only the two I was told about, I would have left the identical defect on a route that *removes* nodes rather than adds them. Grep the sentence, not the ticket.
- ⭐ The part I thought hardest about is what to write in the book. "The docs were wrong, the code is right" is true and useless to an operator. What they need is the consequence and the lever: granting `tenant_admin` to an agent lets that agent extend the node population, and if you do not want that, withhold the grant — because the route will not refuse agents and should not be made to. Putting a kind check on one endpoint would express authority outside the grant model, where it cannot be versioned, delegated, revoked, or enforced at the other boundaries §16.4 requires. That reasoning belongs in front of the reader, not in a commit message.
- ⚠️ Two cheap wrong turns in the control, both corrected by the database rather than by me re-reading: I guessed a column name (`authz_record_id`; it is `issued_under`) and then guessed the wrong column for the acting principal (`subject_id` is NULL — that pair is for delegation's "on behalf of"). I also deliberately did not assert through `authorization_records.actor`, which is a v5 UUID derived from the subject handle: checking it would have meant re-deriving a contract in a fixture, which is the third copy `.4.2.6` refused to write.

## 2026-09-13 — The leaf about missed records had a missed number in it

- I opened this to reconcile source-census records against the leaves they route to, and the first thing the census did was contradict the leaf's own text. It said 53 records; there are 131. Fifty-three is how many FILES are in the artifact directory. ⭐ And `MEMORY.md` has said 131 since the very first leaf, so the two numbers have sat in two live documents disagreeing for the whole life of this tree, in the leaf whose entire subject is facts that get written down and then not re-read.
- 🔴 The instrument was wrong four times before it was right, and I am recording all four because the sequence is what makes the final number believable. It found 70 of 131 (the artifacts use two record-id shapes and my regex knew one). It let parent sections swallow their children, which would have made every split leaf look reconciled. It matched citations on the record's heading line when the tree's convention points at whatever line the author was reading. And after all that, `.4.2`'s fifteen explicit citations still read as UNCITED, because the tree writes runs with the filename elided — `` `census-2.md:28`, `:49`, `:56` `` — and a bare `:49` inherits the name to its left.
- ⭐ Only the last of those was caught by a control run against the REAL tree rather than a fixture I wrote. The other three came from controls asserting properties I could state in advance (a population size, a parent not containing its child's commit id). That is a useful split to notice: fixtures catch the errors you can imagine, and the real corpus catches the conventions you did not know existed.
- ⚠️ One of my controls was also wrong before the code was — I asserted `False` where `True` was right, then had to work out which of the two was confused. Restating it against a record in the *earlier* file made the intent testable: a wrong inheritance now shows up as a false positive instead of being masked by the right answer.
- 🔴 The measurement killed the gate this leaf was opened to consider. 114 of 131 records are cited by nobody, so a citation gate fires on 114 things the day it is registered. That is not a gate. What I did not expect is that the census changed the *question*: the fan-out is a median of four to five candidate leaves per record, which means the reviewer was writing a suggestion list, not making assignments. So "a leaf must account for every record routed to it" was never a sound rule — most routings were speculative, and the biggest uncited counts are the big family containers nobody intended to cite a record each.
- ⭐ Which reframes the defect that started all this. It was never "`.4.1` forgot to cite a record". It is that one record held three findings and only one of them found an owner. The unit is a clause with an owner, not a record with a citation — and no grep can tell "handled without citing" from "dropped", because a clause a leaf considered and rejected is covered too. That is why the 114 go to their own leaf as a backlog to classify rather than into this commit as a number I would have been tempted to call a defect count.
- ⚠️ Cheap self-inflicted one: I ran a debug `python3 -c` without `-B`, which wrote `scripts/__pycache__/`, `git add -A` staged it, and the self-test gate found the `.pyc`, decided it was an instrument, and tried to run it as a shell script. The gate was right to refuse. It is ignored now, with the reason in `.gitignore` rather than in my head.

## 2026-09-13 — I built the instrument, and the instrument caught me twice

- I went after this leaf because the `MEMORY.md` byte cap had cost me real content on four commits in a row today — one eviction per commit, each time choosing which warning to drop by feel. That is exactly what `.3.4.3`'s annotation predicted would happen, so measuring it was overdue rather than a detour.
- ⛔ And the measurement said the worry was wrong. Twenty-six warnings, zero of them existing only in `MEMORY.md`. I had been assuming, along with the annotation, that eviction was quietly destroying knowledge. It is not: every evicted sentence's substance is in a leaf or a knowledge record. ⭐ The project's own pattern held again — the obvious rule was rejected by the census that preceded it, and this is the second time a "surely we should gate this" turned out to be a gate that would fire never or fire wrongly.
- 🔴 The part I want to keep is how badly the instrument behaved on the way. First version: split at every marker, 31 — over-counting, because `🔴 DELEGATION IS ONE HOP DEEP: ⛔ do not fix it` is one warning with two markers. Second version: split only after a sentence terminator, 21 — under-counting, because this repository writes `**… clamp.**` and the full stop is *inside* the bold, so three pairs of distinct warnings silently merged. ⚠️ **Both versions passed their own `--self-test`.** I wrote the fixtures and the regex in the same sitting and in the same idiom, so they agreed with each other perfectly and with the file not at all. `TOOLBOX.md` says this in as many words and I still did it.
- ⭐ What caught it was printing the list and reading it against a file I happened to know. That is the disagreeing second route, and it cost thirty seconds. The lesson is not "write better self-tests" — it is that a self-test cannot be the only check on an instrument whose job is to measure something you can also see.
- ⛔ Then the tool lied in its output, and that was worse than the count being wrong. It printed `ORPHAN (only in MEMORY.md): 13`, which is a claim about documentation coverage that I had not checked. When I checked, all thirteen were documented. The tool had published a search population as a defect count — the exact anti-pattern in `CLAIM_VERIFICATION.md`, committed by me, in a tool written to enforce rigour. The verdict is now called `UNCITED` and the output says outright that it is a population to classify, with the 13-of-13 result printed underneath so the next reader inherits the classification instead of re-deriving it.
- ⚠️ Two of my classification greps also returned `NONE` because I wrote `\|` for alternation under `grep -E`, where it means a literal pipe. Both facts were actually present. A `NONE` from a badly written search is indistinguishable from a real absence, and I only noticed because I knew the tree contained one of them. That is not a method, it is luck, and the defence is what it always is: check a negative result a second way before believing it.
- ⭐ The outcome I am happiest with is that nothing got gated. The real finding — 13 of 26 warnings name no leaf, so eviction costs findability rather than facts — has a tempting rule attached, and that rule would flag the frontier note, the knowledge-map pointer and the environment rule, all three of which are correct as they stand. So the deliverable is the instrument plus one sentence in `TOOLBOX.md` about when to run it: before evicting, prefer shedding a warning that names its leaf. The judgement stays human; the input to it is now derived.

## 2026-09-13 — The leaf was wrong, the claim was wrong, and only one of those mattered

- This leaf opened with "node keys are read straight from `node_certificates`/`node_keys`" and that is simply not true today — there are two `SELECT`s of key material in the whole workspace, one is the secret store itself and the other is a test fixture. ⚠️ The census record it came from was written at the review baseline and is explicitly historical evidence, not a currency claim; the tree says so in general terms and this is the first time I have met a concrete instance. Worth noting that the correct response was not "the leaf is bogus, close it" — the leaf pointed at the right module, and looking there for the wrong reason still found something.
- 🔴 What it found is better than what it was looking for. The store routes the CA material READ. It does not route the WRITE: the bootstrap `INSERT`s into `server_ca` directly and then reads back through the store with an `expect`. That is fine while there is one profile, because both ends are the same database. It is an abort-on-first-boot the moment there are two. ⭐ And that makes the boldest published sentence — "the external store arrives as a configuration change, not a code migration" — precisely backwards: the registry's entire promise breaks at the first use of the thing it promises.
- ⛔ I did not widen the code, and I want the reason on record because "implement the write path" was the tempting answer. There is one declared profile. A write path for a backend that does not exist cannot be tested against that backend, so I would have been shipping untestable generality and calling it a repair. `.4.2.6` made the same point from the other side: a control that cannot discriminate is worse than no control.
- ⭐ The part I am pleased with is the shape of the narrowing. My first draft was a paragraph in the module doc saying "careful, the creation path is not routed". That is a note asking to be remembered, and the whole reason this leaf exists is that a previous note was not. So the warning became a test that pins the profile count, with the explanation inside the assertion message. Adding a second profile now fails with the exact sentence the implementer needs. The comment describes the problem; the test makes it unskippable.
- ⚠️ I corrected the decision record in place rather than superseding it, and kept the original wording visible in the correction. The DECISION was right — the store is a declared profile, an undeclared name refuses. Only two scope sentences overreached. Replacing the text would have hidden that someone wrote "every secret read" when they meant "the CA read", and that overstatement is the thing worth being able to see later.

## 2026-09-13 — Six copies were wrong and the seventh told me why

- The leaf named one panicking decoder in the node. I grepped for the shape rather than the name before starting, and found seven hand-rolled hex decoders across the repo. Six index `&s[i..i + 2]`; one works over `as_bytes().chunks(2)`. ⭐ Then the part I did not expect: the safe one is the decoder that handles the handshake's `cert_der` — the only one an unauthenticated caller can reach. Somebody was careful exactly where they were worried, and every copy nobody was worried about kept the naive form. That is a map of where review attention has been spent, and I have not seen a census hand me one before.
- ⚠️ I nearly wrote the control for the wrong condition. "Non-ASCII input panics" is what the finding says and it is false: `"éé"` returns a clean `Err`, because the character starts at an even offset. The panic needs an even BYTE length — which is what sneaks it past the odd-length guard — whose midpoint lands inside a character. I put both facts into the test as assertions on the input before the decode, so if someone edits the fixture string the control fails loudly rather than passing for the wrong reason.
- ⭐ The detail I keep coming back to: the call site was already correct. `from_hex(&parsed.cert_der).map_err(ChannelError::Malformed)?`. The author wrote the typed refusal one line from the abort. Nobody forgot to handle the error — the function's signature promised an error it could not always produce, and the handling was dead code on exactly the input it existed for. That reframes what "check the error handling" means: the question is not whether callers handle `Err`, it is whether the body can return something that is neither `Ok` nor `Err`.
- ⛔ The server's copy was the interesting decision. It was `pub`, it had no caller, and I had already fixed it before I asked whether it should exist. Repairing it would have shipped a corrected path that no control can reach — and worse, a dead well-named public decoder sitting beside a private correct one is precisely what the next person reaches for. I deleted it. What made me comfortable doing that to a public item was not my grep: I made it private and let the compiler say `function from_hex is never used`. That is an instrument with no loyalty to my search pattern, and it is the cheapest oracle I have used all day.
- ⚠️ I did NOT touch the four test-local copies, and I wrote down why rather than leaving it to be re-discovered. They decode hex the server produced two lines earlier in the same test; a panic there is a loud test failure, not a product defect, and "fix" would have been four edits that no control could falsify. What is actually worth doing is one shared decoder instead of seven — which is a cross-crate refactor, and putting it in this commit would have been scope I was not asked for.

## 2026-09-13 — I nearly graded this one on the wrong axis

- The census sentence for this leaf was "a session fenced by a newer handshake can still mark another session's delivery acknowledged", and read on its own that sounds like a bookkeeping wart. I went looking for who actually CONSUMES `acknowledged_at` before deciding how much it mattered, and the answer changed the severity completely: the retention prune deletes on it. The stale ack is not a wrong record, it is a licence to destroy work the live session is still holding. ⭐ The general point I want to keep: a write's severity is a property of its READERS, and you find that by enumerating them, not by looking harder at the write.
- ⚠️ The fixture problem here was more interesting than the repair. The repair MOVES a lock acquisition — the ack now takes the lease row it never touched before — so the natural reproduction (stall the write, land a handshake) behaves completely differently under the two designs: unrepaired the handshake sails past, repaired it queues behind the ack. A fixture written for one is not a fixture for the other, and a naive assertion would have been either vacuous or wrong.
- ⭐ What made it work was holding the lease row myself and letting the difference between the designs BE the observation: unrepaired the ack finishes while I hold it, repaired it waits. A bounded timeout on the in-flight ack distinguishes those two, so one fixture reads both designs. That felt like the right shape rather than a trick — the thing I actually want to assert is "does this write ask about the lease at all", and that question has a yes/no answer under a held row.
- 🔴 And then I nearly claimed arm 2 was load-bearing without proving it. Arm 2 sits AFTER arm 1's assertion, so when I falsified the repair, arm 1 panicked first and arm 2 never ran — I had a falsification that said nothing about half my control. Softening arm 1 for a single run showed arm 2 goes red on its own (`nothing ever blocked on FOR UPDATE`). Cheap, and without it I would have written "both arms are load-bearing" on the evidence of one.
- ⛔ The decision I am most comfortable with is the one where I changed nothing. `poll` shares the same pre-check and the leaf explicitly left open whether it needed the same treatment. Reading its three statements settles it: they are all reads, and a fenced poll leaves no trace at all — the rows are still there for the next session. Wrapping it would have put a row lock on the channel's most frequent call to buy an invariant that does not exist. Writing down *why* it was excluded felt more valuable than the exclusion, because the next person to read `ack` and `poll` side by side will ask exactly this.
- ⚠️ One part of this commit has no control and I said so in the leaf rather than inventing one: moving the cursor read into the transaction. Nothing can falsify it, because a stale bound is permissive rather than unsafe. Its justification is that using the pool inside an open transaction takes a second connection per request, which is a real cost and a real deadlock shape under load — but it is an argument, not a test, and labelling it as such is the honest version.

## 2026-09-13 — The third condition, and the answer that depended on the timing

- This is the last of the three conditions `renew_lease` was checking outside its own write. `.2.2` folded in the epoch, `.4.1.3` folded in the credential, and the expiry was simply never revisited — each repair fixed the condition it came for and nobody asked what else was still on the wrong side of the line. Worth remembering as a shape: when a repair moves ONE predicate from a pre-check into a write, the other predicates in that pre-check are now the interesting ones, and the leaf that moved the first is the cheapest place to notice.
- ⭐ The reproduction was easier than the last two and I want to record why, because it was not luck. `.4.2.1` needed a lock on a table the other path never touches, and its real gap could not be stalled at all. Here the two racing statements touch the SAME row, and they take different lock strengths — a plain `SELECT` that ignores a row lock, then an `UPDATE` that cannot. So holding the row separates them exactly: the pre-check passes, the write queues, and `blocked_on` proves the write is queued before I commit the lapse. The asymmetry in the locking IS the widening mechanism, and it was free.
- ⚠️ The part I nearly shipped without a control was the refusal wording. The invariant assertion — the lease must still be dead — passes whether or not the classifier names the lapse, so I had a repair part with nothing that could falsify it. `proving-a-race-is-closed.md` warns about presenting a control that cannot fail as proof of a repair; this was the milder version, a repair part with no control at all. Adding one assertion turned the second falsification from "green, apparently harmless" into `the fencing token was refused — re-handshake`, which is the wrong answer to a question about an expiry.
- 🔴 And that wrong answer is not cosmetic. A node told its token was refused re-handshakes; a node told its credential is gone does not. Those happen to share a remedy here — but the point is that the wire answer was a function of *how long the write waited*, and a contract that changes with the scheduler is not a contract. Same question, same answer, whatever the timing.
- ⭐ The clock choice is the decision I spent longest on and it is the one I would defend hardest. The obvious move is to compare against the same `Utc::now()` the pre-check used — it makes the check and the write agree, which is what the leaf was about. I went the other way: the statement already reads `now()` for the certificate, and `node_presence` derives `online` from this exact column on the database clock. Making the write agree with the PUBLISHED FACT is worth more than making it agree with the check it replaces, because the check is internal and the presence surface is what an operator acts on. A renewal succeeding for a node the API reports offline would have been a new defect wearing the old one's clothes.
- ⚠️ What I did NOT do: fix the column's own clock. `lease_expires_at` is written process-side and read database-side, so the 60 s TTL carries the skew. It was tempting — it is four lines — but changing the writer changes what the handshake returns to the node, and I have no fixture that can separate two clocks on one host, so I would have been shipping an unfalsifiable change on top of a falsified one. Opened as `.4.2.3.1` with the measurement, and put the limit in the book where a reader will actually meet it.

## 2026-09-13 — The test did not fail, it hung, and that is a different thing

- The reproduction here is the most alarming output I have produced in this tree. Replaying a node's own captured handshake bytes returns `200`, takes a fresh lease, and then the *real* node's next heartbeat answers `401`. The node is evicted from its own session by a replay of a request it sent. And the rotation is worse: the same captured bytes, sent twice, return two different private keys for the same identity.
- ⭐ The decision was easier than I expected once I wrote the alternatives down. A server challenge is the textbook answer and costs a round trip on every handshake and rotation. A timestamp with a skew window is the other textbook answer, and I rejected it on this repository's own evidence: three separate cross-clock defects have been repaired here in the last few days, and putting a clock on the authentication path after that would be choosing the failure mode we have been paying for. A per-request nonce needs neither, and enrollment tokens already work this way.
- ⛔ The option I liked most and had to drop was the cheapest: consume the *certificate*, one rotation per certificate, no new table at all. It fails for a reason that has nothing to do with replay — a node whose rotate response was lost retries with the same certificate, and that retry is byte-identical to a replay. The rule would have quietly broken the recovery path §17.4 exists to protect. A nonce distinguishes them for free, because a retry generates a new one.
- 🔴 Then I deadlocked the product with my own migration. I gave the nonce table a foreign key to `nodes`, forgetting that the rotation now holds `nodes … FOR UPDATE` from the repair I landed an hour earlier. The foreign-key check on the nonce insert wants a KEY SHARE lock on that row. The rotation waited for itself.
- ⚠️ **The way it showed up is the part worth keeping: the suite did not go red, it HUNG.** `cargo test` printed nothing, the task sat there, and neither a pass nor a fail was available to read. I went to the process table and there it was in one line — `postgres: … INSERT waiting` next to `idle in transaction`. Ten seconds of `ps` beat any amount of re-reading the diff. A hanging suite is a third outcome beside red and green and it has its own diagnosis.
- ⭐ The fix was not to work around the lock but to ask what the foreign key was buying. Nothing: these rows are transient, nothing joins them, and `node_id` only scopes the prune. And the key had a second cost I had not noticed — even without the deadlock, it would have made every handshake queue behind any in-flight revocation of that node. Dropping it removed a coupling I never intended and would not have found by testing, because no test asks "does a handshake block during a revocation?".

## 2026-09-13 — The foreign key made the race worse, and my own test lied to me

- The census said a rotation could race a revocation. My first reproduction held the old certificate's row so the revocation would block mid-`UPDATE`, and it came back `500` — the revocation's own lock timeout. **Wrong reproduction, and the reason it was wrong is the best thing I learned today.** The rotation's insert was itself blocked behind the revocation's `SELECT … FROM nodes … FOR UPDATE`, through the foreign key from `node_certificates` to `nodes`. I had built a three-way wait.
- ⭐ Once I saw that, the finding got worse rather than better. The foreign key does not *prevent* the insert from landing after the revocation — it **guarantees** it. A rotation that overlaps a revocation does not sometimes survive it; it waits for the revocation to commit and then inserts a live certificate. The FK turned a race into a reliable outcome, in the wrong direction.
- The second reproduction stalls the rotation at its host lookup with an exclusive lock on `hosts`, which I checked the revocation path never touches rather than assuming it. That one is clean: revocation `200`, rotation ALLOWED, one live certificate on a revoked node.
- ⭐ The repair is the part I am happiest with, because it invents nothing. The revocation already takes `SELECT … FROM nodes … FOR UPDATE` — its comment even says "locked before the change". Making the rotation take the same row in the same mode, first, and re-read the certificate after, serializes them with no new lock, no new guard and no migration. Both orders then come out right, which is a better shape than making one of them lose.
- ⛔ I nearly used the `.4.1.3` trick — fold the liveness into the `INSERT` — because it had just worked twice. It does not work here, and the reason is worth writing down: READ COMMITTED takes the statement's snapshot at statement *start*, and the foreign-key wait happens after the row is already formed, so the `EXISTS` would be evaluated against a snapshot that predates the revocation's commit. A window *inside* one statement can be closed by folding; a window *between* two statements cannot.
- 🔴 Then my own control lied to me, and only the falsification caught it. I removed the row lock expecting red and got **green**. The reproduction arm stalls the rotation at the host lookup, which my repair had made the first statement — so reordering alone was enough for that fixture and the lock was doing nothing the test could see. `proving-a-race-is-closed.md` says this in as many words: a control that passes against the superseded design is not testing the repair. I had read that record this morning and still walked into it.
- ⚠️ What made it hard is that **no fixture can stall the rotation in the real gap** — nothing between the proof check and the insert touches the database, so there is no lock to hold. The answer was to stop trying to reproduce the window and assert the mechanism instead: hold the node row, and require the rotation to be observed waiting on it in `pg_stat_activity`. That arm goes red the moment the lock is removed, which is what an honest control does.

## 2026-09-13 — The decision was easy; where to put the check was the whole leaf

- I expected `.4.1.2` to be an argument about bearer credentials and it barely was. This project has answered the underlying question twice already — `.3.5.2` and `.4.1.3` — and written the answer down both times: withdrawn authority stops producing effects. Once I put it that way the decision made itself. **The leaf's real content was the second question, which its own title does not ask: WHERE does the check go?**
- ⭐ The title says "redemption does not re-check the issuer's authority", and for a while I was going to make redemption re-check it. Then I looked at what `enroll` actually holds: nothing. No tenant authority guard. Reading `authority_grants.status` there is reading authority state outside the guard a revocation holds exclusively, which is precisely the bug `.3.3.4.5` repaired on the node-result path. Making it sound would mean putting a tenant guard on the node-facing enrollment route, which it has never had.
- ⭐ Voiding at revocation time instead costs nothing, and the reason is a nice one: that transaction already holds the exclusive guard, and the token row is already the redemption's *declared* serialization point — the code says so in a comment. So the two contend on the row and the ordering falls out. A redemption that got there first commits, and the node it makes is separately revocable, which is only true because `.4.1.3.1` made revoking a node actually work last week. Repairs compounding is a nice thing to notice.
- 🔴 The moment I am most glad about happened before I wrote the migration. The one-live-token index reads `used_at IS NULL AND superseded_at IS NULL`, and a voided token satisfies both — so a voided token would have held its node's slot for ever, and an operator who revoked a compromised administrator could never issue a replacement. **That is `.4.1.1`'s lockout, re-entered through a door I was building.** I only saw it because `.4.1.1` is recent and its shape was in my head. The control now asserts the slot is freed, and neutralising the index key returns `.4.1.1`'s error message verbatim, which is about as clear a demonstration as a test can give.
- ⚠️ Then the fixture census humbled me twice in a row. The new foreign key means `node_enrollment_tokens` must be purged before `authorization_records`, and the project's checked cleanup plan said so by failing every test in a suite at once. My first census found only files holding *both* table names — and missed three that purge the parent and never mention the child. My assertion then stopped the script on two of those three, which turned out not to be cleanup plans at all (a row-count assertion and a pre-upgrade schema snapshot); without the assertion I would have edited two files for no reason and been confused later.
- ⚠️ The second miss is the one worth remembering. I scoped the reorder to `crates/reasonbraid-cli/tests` and `crates/reasonbraid-server/tests` because that is where test fixtures live. One lives in `crates/reasonbraid-mcp/src/lib.rs`. **A census scoped to the directories you expect is not a census** — and it survived until the 44th suite of 44, which is exactly why a migration plus twenty-seven shared fixture lists does not get a selective run.

## 2026-09-13 — I found the bug by checking whether the previous leaf's argument was true

- `.4.1.2` is a decision leaf, not a defect leaf, and I expected to spend the session writing an argument. Its bound reads: "a token names one node id and one host claim, is single-use, and **expires**". I went to check the expiry — not because I suspected anything, but because the whole argument rests on it — and found `chrono::Duration::seconds(req.ttl_seconds.unwrap_or(3600))`, the caller's own `i64`, unvalidated. **Checking the premise of an argument is a way of finding bugs, and I had not been thinking of it that way.**
- 🔴 Then the measurement corrected me twice. I predicted one panic; there are two, at different sites, reachable by different callers. And the one I thought was the mild case turned out to be the severe one: `i64::MAX` panics in `TimeDelta::seconds`, which runs at the handler's call site — **in front of the authority guard**, because `resolve_principal` only parses the header. The two middling values I had expected to be the dangerous ones answered a correct `403`, because `at + ttl` lives inside the guard and the denial returns first. If I had written this up from the source reading I would have described the wrong failure, with the wrong reachability, and probably fixed it in the wrong place.
- ⭐ The third result is the one I would have missed entirely by only probing for panics: a century was just **issued**. `200`, `expires_at: 2126-09-14`. No error, no warning, a single-use bearer credential outliving everyone who could reason about it. Probing for crashes finds crashes; the quiet `200` is what actually undermines the leaf next door.
- ⚠️ The fixture decision took the longest and I nearly got it wrong. Three controls mint a lapsed token with `ttl_seconds: -1`, and my cap broke all three. My first instinct was to allow negatives. Then I read `.4.1.1`'s comment, which says *why* it used the route instead of a test-side `UPDATE`: the lapse must happen through the product's own expiry, never through a write to the very row under measurement. That reason is right and it is not the same thing as the mechanism. So I kept the reason and replaced the mechanism — issue at the shortest legal lifetime, wait on the database's own clock. **The row state that results is identical to production's**, which is the test that the replacement is honest rather than convenient.
- 🔎 The finding I did not want.
