- ⭐ **The reason it survived is the part worth keeping.** Three of this project's own fixtures seeded a node into a fixed seed tenant and then administered it as an administrator of a different, freshly bootstrapped tenant. They passed. They had to pass — the verbs ignored the tenant, so the mismatch was invisible, and every one of those tests was *asserting a feature while depending on the defect*. Adding the predicate turned all three red in the same second.
- That is a specific failure mode of fixture design, not of the code: a fixture that supplies a value the production path never reads cannot tell you the path is wrong, and it quietly becomes a reason the path stays wrong. The ones that did NOT depend on it — the other three suites driving the same verbs — had nodes that genuinely belonged to the administering tenant, so they were unaffected. The difference between the two groups is exactly whether the fixture told the truth about ownership.
- What I would look for next time, and did here: when a census says a predicate is missing, check whether the tests supply a value for it that *differs* from the one the route is given. If they do, the tests are the evidence that nothing reads it.
- ⚠️ Correcting rather than deleting them mattered. Each of the three asserts something real — the quarantine skip, the typed refusals, the retention window — and those assertions are intact. Only the ownership changed.

## 2026-09-12 — Three leaves, three fixtures, and the third one is the reason to keep asking

- `.9` took the exclusive guard and a SHARED-holder fixture discriminated it. `.10.1` took the shared guard and NO holder discriminated it, so atomicity carried the falsification. `.10.2` took the exclusive guard again — and the shared-holder fixture works again, for the same reason it did at `.9`.
- ⭐ The thing worth writing down is that the right fixture is a function of the TRANSITION, not of the leaf, the family, or the mode the repair ends in. What decides it is: did the operation's lock mode change, and in which direction? Moving shared → exclusive makes a shared holder discriminating. Staying shared → shared makes every holder useless. The mode the repair ends in tells you nothing on its own; `.9` and `.10.2` both end exclusive and got there from the same shared admission, which is why they share a fixture — and `.10.1` ends shared and shares nothing with either.
- The failure it caught reads exactly as the defect: `the revocation is waiting` — the assertion that the request has not finished — failing, because under the superseded shape the request had already completed while another operation held the tenant's guard. The admission took the shared mode, which does not exclude shared, and the mutation took no guard at all.
- I wrote this note rather than a third knowledge record because the durable statement is already promoted: `proving-a-race-is-closed.md` gained the mode-derivation questions and the no-fixture-discriminates case one commit ago. This is its third confirming instance, and a lesson that keeps being confirmed does not need to be re-promoted — it needs the leaf to say it was applied, which `.10.2` does.

## 2026-09-12 — The previous leaf's answer was the wrong default for this one

- Two leaves ago I derived, carefully, that breaker administration needs the tenant's EXCLUSIVE guard, and wrote down why. One leaf later the obvious move was to reach for the same mode again — three administrative families in a row, one shape. Re-deriving instead produced the opposite answer: token issuance takes the SHARED guard.
- ⭐ The reasons are specific, and that is what makes them checkable. `.9` needed exclusion because classifying `applied` against `no_op` was a read-then-write over a row that might not exist, and a row lock cannot cover an absent row. Issuance has no such classification: one insert that does nothing on conflict returns the token or returns nothing, and that IS the decision. `.9` also wanted ordering against in-flight reservations, which hold the shared guard; issuance touches nothing the reservation path reads. Neither reason survived the move, so neither should the mode.
- The cost of getting this wrong is invisible in tests and real in production: an exclusive guard on a token issuance blocks every concurrent thread command in that tenant for the life of the transaction, to buy an invariant that nothing needs.
- ⚠️ And it changes what a falsification can establish. `.9`'s lesson was that an exclusive-holder fixture cannot discriminate a repair whose old shape admitted under a shared guard. Here it is stronger: the repair ITSELF takes the shared guard, so NEITHER holder mode discriminates anything. The property that actually changed is atomicity — the token and its evidence are one commit — so the evidence-rollback control is the only discriminating one, and it reports `left: 200, right: 500` on the old shape. The ordering control is a regression control and is labelled as one in the suite. A control that cannot fail is not evidence, and saying which of my controls is which is part of the evidence.

## 2026-09-12 — A constraint violation is not a refusal I can record

- Issuance's established contract is a typed `409` when an unused token for that node is already outstanding, produced by letting a partial unique index raise and catching `is_unique_violation`. Moving the route onto one transaction made that unworkable in a way that has nothing to do with error handling: in PostgreSQL a constraint violation ABORTS the transaction, so every statement after it fails until rollback.
- ⭐ The consequence is the interesting part. The refusal has to COMMIT — the admission and the `refused` effect record are the evidence that an admitted caller asked for something impossible — and a raised violation makes committing anything impossible. The catch-and-translate shape and the one-transaction shape are incompatible, not merely awkward together.
- `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING RETURNING` is the resolution: zero rows returned IS the refusal, with no abort, so the same 409 now arrives with its outcome durably recorded beside it. The wire is byte-identical and the transaction survives.
- The generalisation for `.10.2` and `.10.3`: any administrative refusal that a database constraint currently produces by raising has to be converted to a value before it can be recorded as an outcome. That is a shape to look for in each remaining family, not a one-off.

## 2026-09-12 — My ordering control was green against the code it was written to catch

- The repair was "put the operation under the tenant's exclusive guard". The control looked like the obvious one and was copied from the leaf before it: hold the tenant's guard EXCLUSIVELY, start the request, assert it waits, end the caller's authority underneath it, release, assert 403. Green. It was also green against the **unrepaired** code, which is the part I nearly shipped as evidence.
- ⭐ **The reason is a one-word property of the fixture.** The superseded shape admitted through `authorize_guarded`, which takes the tenant's **shared** guard — and shared waits behind exclusive. So an exclusive holder fenced the old design exactly as well as it fenced the new one. The fixture could not distinguish them while looking, in every visible respect, like a passing race control.
- The discriminating fence is the opposite mode. A **shared** holder excludes the repair's exclusive acquisition and does not exclude the old shape's shared one. Changing `GuardMode::Exclusive` to `GuardMode::Shared` in two fixtures turned "green either way" into `left: 200, right: 403` — the arm applying after the caller's administration had already ended.
- ⚠️ The generalisation, and it is due immediately: `.10`–`.12` migrate three more administrative families off the SAME `authorize_tenant_admin` shape. Any ordering control copied from `.8`'s exclusive-holder fixture will pass against the unrepaired code. When a repair changes WHICH lock an operation takes, the fixture must hold a lock the OLD mode was not excluded by — so ask what the superseded code acquired, rather than copying the holder from the previous leaf.
- The deeper habit this corrects: I chose the holder's mode by analogy ("the last leaf held exclusive") rather than by derivation ("what did the old code acquire, and what excludes that?"). The knowledge note already said an invariant control must fail against the superseded design; it did not say that the fixture's own parameters are part of what has to be derived. It does now.

## 2026-09-12 — A deliberate fault is a fixture, and a fixture that leaks is worse than one that fails

- The evidence-rollback control needed every effect insert to fail, so it added `CHECK (false) NOT VALID` to the table, ran the request, dropped the constraint, and asserted. The assertion order was: assert, then drop. The first assertion was wrong, the test panicked, the constraint stayed — and the next eleven tests in the suite failed on a constraint that had nothing to do with any of them.
- ⭐ The failure LIST diagnosed it before any log was read: a contiguous block of unrelated tests failing at their own first database call is a fixture fault, not eleven regressions. That is the same shape rule that caught a false falsification two leaves ago, applied to a different mechanism.
- Two repairs, because one was not enough. The control now **observes everything, drops the fault, and only then asserts**, so no assertion can fire while the fault stands. And the suite's own `pool()` fixture issues `DROP CONSTRAINT IF EXISTS` at setup, so if a future edit reintroduces the ordering mistake it costs one test instead of the suite. A fixture that installs a global fault owns its removal on every path, including the panicking one.

## 2026-09-12 — The defect was the gap between two transactions, and the control had to live in it

- The route already took an exclusive guard. It already locked the target row. It already bumped the epoch in the same transaction as the status change. Reading it, almost everything looked right — and a revocation could still apply on authority that had stopped existing, because the ADMISSION was a different transaction that had already committed. The bug was not in either transaction; it was in the seam.
- ⭐ **A defect that lives between two correct things is invisible to a reader and obvious to a control that waits.** The control holds the tenant's exclusive guard, starts the revocation, ends the caller's own administration underneath it, and releases. On the shipped code that is 403 with the target untouched; on the pre-`.8` shape it is 200 and the grant is gone. Nothing about reading either transaction tells you which one you have.
- That is also why I ran the pre-`.8` shape as an injection rather than trusting the argument. `left: 200, right: 403` is the whole leaf in one line, and it is a line I could not have written from the source.
- The removal I nearly skipped: `issuance::revoke_grant` and `issuance::revoke_boundary` had no caller left, and leaving them would have cost nothing today. But a guarded function that can revoke a row without an admission is a loaded gun with the safety off — the next handler to need "just revoke this" would find it, and it would look sanctioned because it takes a guard. Deleted, with a comment where they were saying what replaced them and why.
- ⚠️ **My own control had a wrong expectation and the control is what caught it.** I asserted the epoch was 1 after the fixture's raw `UPDATE` ended the caller's authority — reasoning about what a revocation does, not about what SQL does. SQL bumps nothing; the application does. The correct value is 0, and 0 is the stronger assertion, because any advance could only have come from the blocked request. A test expectation is a claim like any other and deserves the same scepticism as the code's.
- The receipt header was the one addition I had to argue myself INTO rather than out of. It looked like scope creep on a leaf that was already large. Then I asked how an operator would ever read the effect record this leaf writes, and the answer was: they could not. There is no list endpoint and the id is the admission's, which the response did not return. Evidence nobody can reach is not evidence.
- promotion: declined (the durable statement is the ordering table in the book's authority chapter, which now names a control for every row, plus the four controls themselves; the general method — assert the invariant, then watch it fail against the build that lacks it — is already promoted as `docs/knowledge/proving-a-race-is-closed.md`).

## 2026-09-12 — I reused the existing registry for exactly the right reason and it was the wrong set

- One leaf ago I wrote that the effect's refusal code should be a `KnownReasonCode` so that "the record and the response cannot disagree about which refusal happened". I still think that is the correct requirement. The type I picked to enforce it made it impossible: the response for a missing target says `not_found`, and the §9.8 registry does not have `not_found`. I discovered this by typing `KnownReasonCode::NotFound` and watching it not exist.
- ⭐ **"Reuse the existing registry" is an architectural instinct, not a measurement, and it needs the same census as any other rule over a population.** `.11.6` exists because five rules reasoned from source were changed by their first measurement. This is the sixth, and the thing that makes it worth writing down is that it is mine, in the leaf immediately after I wrote the census discipline into a different enum and congratulated myself for it. The discipline was applied to the operation set and not to the field sitting next to it.
- The second instinct was worse and I nearly acted on it: "then add `NotFound` to the registry". That fix assumes the gap is one value. It is ten — and eleven registry codes are never emitted at all. A one-value patch would have restored my invariant locally while leaving a client that reads the published registry unable to name half of what it receives, and nothing would have been written down.
- The census that decided the replacement is the one I should have run first: parse each of the 14 administrative handlers' bodies and classify their `ControlApiError::` constructors. Three are domain refusals of an admitted operation; `unauthorized` is the admission's own job; the internal ones roll back instead of committing. Three values, each of which is literally the string in the response body. The invariant now holds by construction, which is what I thought I was buying the first time.
- ⛔ **A finding bigger than the leaf that found it gets routed, not absorbed.** The registry drift is a real defect with a measured shape, and folding its repair into an authority leaf would have buried it. It is `.11.7` with the two-way set difference attached, and with "measured and deliberately divergent" left available as a legitimate outcome — because I have not yet established that all ten codes *should* be registry members.
- The cheap structural win: no migration. The stored `outcome` column's `CHECK` pins only the `kind` discriminant, and the refusal code lives beneath it. That is the layering from the previous leaf paying for itself one day later — the schema constrains the coarse thing, the codec owns the fine thing, and a fine-grained correction costs a core change and nothing else.
- promotion: declined (the durable statements are already promoted — `docs/knowledge/where-an-invariant-lives.md` covers the layering that made this correction cheap, and `.11.6` is the tracked home for the measure-before-proposing pattern, which this leaf extends with a sixth dated instance rather than restating).

## 2026-09-12 — My negative build was broken, not weakened, and the failure list said so

- The falsification looked right: three injections, run the suite, watch it go red. It went red — **0 passed / 8 failed**. For about ten seconds that felt like a strong result. Then the list: `an_absent_effect_reads_as_absent` failed. That control does not touch a foreign key, a `CHECK`, or the writer. Nothing I injected could reach it.
- ⭐ **A negative build proves something only when the controls it should NOT affect keep passing.** Everything failing is the signature of a broken fixture, and a broken fixture is indistinguishable from a perfect repair when all you look at is the exit code. My `CHECK` deletion had left an orphaned `false)` and migration 0058 would not apply, so every test died at the same fixture line before touching any behaviour.
- The corrected injections keep the SQL valid — `DROP` the constraint, `IS NOT NULL` instead of the `IN` list — and gave **5 passed / 3 failed**, with each failure attributable to exactly one injection by test name. That is the result worth recording, and the difference between the two runs is entirely about whether the negative build was still a build.
- The design decision I spent longest on was where each invariant lives. "An effect belongs to its admission's tenant" could be a writer that always passes the right tenant, a decoder that rechecks it, or a composite foreign key. The first two are conventions; the third makes the wrong row unstorable by anyone, including code nobody has written yet. It costs one extra unique index on the parent and 27 fixture-plan edits, and that cost is the reason to census it BEFORE writing the constraint rather than discovering it as a red gate.
- ⛔ **A decoder check is the weakest place to put a rule, because it runs on the way out.** The row existed. Anything that read it with different code saw the invalid value. So the split I shipped is deliberate: the database constrains the coarse `kind` discriminant, the codec enforces the structure beneath it — and the control asserts exactly that seam, inserting five rows that PASS the column constraint and fail the codec. Without that control the two layers would look like one, and the next person would assume either covered the other.
- ⚠️ **And then the sweep got one wrong anyway, in the way I had specifically tried to prevent.** My predicate was a whole LINE equal to the quoted name and a comma — the shape a plan entry has and an assertion tuple does not — and I wrote in the leaf that no SQL string or prose could match it. True, and irrelevant: `migration_upgrade.rs` has an ARRAY of table names with exactly that shape, snapshotting the pre-upgrade schema at version 55, where the new table does not exist. The upgrade test failed with `relation "administrative_effects" does not exist`.
- ⭐ **A predicate keyed on a line's shape cannot distinguish two arrays; one keyed on the enclosing CALL can.** The re-audit parses every `delete_tables(…)` argument list and asserts each insertion falls inside one — 26 files, 0 outside. That is four lines of Python and it is the difference between "I used a careful grep" and "every edit is in the construct I claimed". I should have written the structural check first, because I already knew the risk well enough to write a sentence about it.
- The thing that actually saved this was running the ADJACENT suites rather than only the new one. The new suite was green through all of it; `migration_upgrade` was the only test on earth that reads those tables at a version where one of them does not exist.
- promotion: promoted → docs/knowledge/where-an-invariant-lives.md, five indexed answers.

## 2026-09-12 — The enum I was about to write was a rule, and I had not measured its population

- The leaf asked for a "closed operation/target/outcome representation". I could have written that set from the four sibling leaves' titles in about ten minutes, and it would have been *almost* right. What stopped me was `.11.6`, which is a tracked leaf whose entire content is five occasions in one session where a rule reasoned carefully from source was changed by the first measurement of what it quantified over. A closed enum is a rule over a population. So I censused first: 27 guarded-admission call sites, classified one by one — 9 reads, 18 mutations, 14 mine, 4 routed elsewhere.
- ⭐ **The census's value was not the 14. It was the 4.** `register_resolver`, `create_thread_auto`, `open_recruitment_call` and `close_call` are all `tenant_admin`-gated mutations, and every instinct says a "closed set of administrative mutations" should contain them. They belong to `.5.2` and `.7.1`, and `.3.3.4.13` says in as many words that this parent cannot certify their gates. Including them would have been a silent scope claim wearing an enum's clothes — and I would have believed it, because nothing about the enum would have looked wrong.
- The cross-check I did not plan and would recommend: the 14 partition as 2+2+5+2+3 across `.8`–`.12`, which is exactly what those four leaves say they own, written months of commits earlier by someone working from a different direction. Two independent derivations agreeing is worth more than either one being careful.
- The design decision I spent longest on was putting the operation and its target in ONE enum instead of two. Two enums read better in a schema diagram and they are a synchronization problem: nothing prevents `breaker_arm` from carrying a `grant_id`, so something has to check it, forever. One enum makes the invalid combination unrepresentable, and the cost is that the DB column is a JSONB object rather than two indexable columns — which costs nothing here, because the readback is by admission id and there is no listing.
- The constraint that actually shaped the variants was asking **when does the target have to exist?** A refusal is a committed outcome, so the target has to be nameable at refusal, not only at success. That immediately killed "the imported card creates a role, so the target is the role" — there is no role when the import refuses. The target is the card digest, because the digest is what the *caller supplied*. "Name what the caller supplied" turned out to be the rule, and it is the one I would reach for first next time.
- ⚠️ **`Option<T>` does not make a serde field required, and I wrote a doc comment saying it did.** The control failed on my own first draft, which is the only reason I know. serde's missing-field path answers `visit_none` for an `Option`, so a dropped member decodes as `None` — indistinguishable from a writer that stated the absence. A `deserialize_with` field has no fallback and the member becomes mandatory. The general shape is worth more than the serde detail and is promoted.
- The smallest thing I am most pleased with: `every_operation()` ends with an exhaustive `match` that does nothing. It exists so that adding a variant without adding a fixture stops the compiler, which turns "all 14 are covered" from a number someone maintains into a structural property. A test that counts is a test that goes stale.
- promotion: promoted → docs/knowledge/a-missing-field-is-not-an-absent-value.md, five indexed answers.

## 2026-09-12 — The leaf's title named one entry point and the code had two

- The leaf said "guard named inspection admissions", and there is a function called `authorize_tenant_admin_inspection` that serves exactly the eight named routes. Everything pointed at one place. What made me look further was the acceptance line — "and other normal standalone read admissions" — which I nearly read as decoration. It is the larger half: `authority::authorize` is the admission behind thread inspection, token issuance, automatic thread creation, recruitment and every `tenant_admin` gate, and it was equally unguarded.
- ⭐ **When a leaf's own acceptance says more than its title, the acceptance is the specification.** Titles are written first and get shorter; acceptance lines are written while the author still remembers the shape of the problem.
- The decision I spent most care on was NOT guarding `authorize` itself. It is `pub`, twenty-odd core controls drive it with a chosen instant, and its explicit timestamp is the thing that makes those controls able to test time at all. Adding a lock inside it would have been one edit instead of six, and it would have quietly changed what that API is for. A second, guarded entry point costs a little duplication and keeps the evaluation-time surface honest — the same split `.3.3.4.4` made for `authorize_in_tx`, which is why I could copy the reasoning instead of re-deriving it.
- ⛔ **A function that opens its own transaction is a deadlock waiting for a caller who already has one**, and the only honest way to say "that does not happen here" is to enumerate. Twenty-eight call sites, one grep each for `pool.begin()`/`transact(`/`TenantTransaction` between the enclosing `fn` and the call. Zero. That census is three lines of Python and it is the difference between a claim and a fact — and it is now in the record so the next person adding a call site knows the property that has to hold.
- The control I would have skipped a year ago is the one that proves the ordering did NOT change policy: a boundary frozen while the read waits must still ADMIT. The carve-out is approved, deliberate and easy to break with a lock that fences "authority changes" without asking which change. A repair that tightens something should carry a control for the thing it must not tighten.
- promotion: declined (the durable statements are the four controls, `authorize_guarded`'s own doc comment naming the nesting bound at the definition where a caller reads it, and the book's authority chapter, which now carries the admission ordering table).

## 2026-09-12 — The cleanup I was about to do was aimed at the smaller half

- The §8 review was due, and I knew exactly what to clean: the compiler cache under `target/debug/incremental`, because that is what the last artifact leaf retired and what everybody means by "the build directory is huge". Then I ran `du -sk target/debug/*`. `deps` is 134 GiB; `incremental` is 59 GiB. The thing I was confident about was the smaller half by more than a factor of two.
- ⭐ **The reason the wrong half felt obvious is that it is the half with a PROCEDURE.** Incremental sessions have a documented lock protocol, a garbage collector inside the compiler, and a prior leaf that worked out how to touch them safely. `deps` has none of that, so nothing had ever written about it — and "nothing has been written about it" reads, from the inside, exactly like "it is not the problem". Availability of method is not evidence of magnitude.
- So the useful output of this leaf was not the 573 MB it removed. It was one `du` that reordered the backlog, and a leaf for the part it is not safe to touch yet.
- On the part I did act on, the check I am most glad about is the **citation guard**: a retained cluster that a tracked file names by id is evidence, and evidence is not rubbish. It costs one `git grep` per candidate and it is the difference between a cleanup tool and a tool that quietly deletes the thing a task record points at. I made it fire in both directions in the self-test, because a guard that always says "no references" is indistinguishable from no guard at all.
- The age floor turned out to matter for a reason I did not design it for. It is there so a run somebody is still reading survives — and what it actually protected was **this session's own two baselines**, which I would otherwise have deleted minutes after recording their numbers, while the commit citing them was not yet written. A time-based bound caught a causal mistake.
- The last decision was to leave `--retire` behind `--confirm` and to re-run every check immediately before each removal. A census is a snapshot; the gap between reading it and acting on it is exactly where a cluster becomes live again. Cheap, and it makes the tool safe to run from a script later.
- promotion: declined (the durable statement IS the tracked instrument and its `--self-test`, now in `TOOLBOX.md`; a method statement about retained clusters is the thing `.11.4.3.1.6` already demonstrated does not survive).

## 2026-09-12 — I wrote the acceptance criterion before I understood what would separate the builds

- The leaf said the control should fail on a build whose decision time is the process clock read before the wait. It was a sentence I believed, written from the shape of the repair rather than from the code's order of operations. The first thing building the control taught me was that `run_thread_command` samples its time AFTER the guard and the idempotency claim, so a process clock read there gives the same verdict as `clock_timestamp()`. The acceptance named a difference that does not exist.
- ⭐ **An acceptance criterion is a prediction about which two builds disagree, and it deserves the same scepticism as any other prediction.** The failure mode is quiet: a control written to that criterion would still have passed, I would have recorded the criterion as met, and the record would have carried a claim about the clock that no run had ever tested. The thing that caught it was not care — it was *actually running the negative build*, which the project already insists on for a different reason (a control never seen RED is not known to work).
- So the negative build is doing double duty, and I had only appreciated one half. It proves the control can fail. It also **identifies what the control is sensitive to**, which is usually not what the author assumed. Here it was the wait, not the clock.
- Cheap and worth repeating: revert-and-re-apply on a single call. Remove `acquire_in_tx`, run, watch the sibling control fail too (which is how I knew the removal had bitten rather than been optimised away), restore with `git checkout --`, and confirm `git diff HEAD` over the file is empty before claiming the shipped bytes are the committed ones.
- The wider lesson about the parent leaf is the uncomfortable one: it published five contract rows and shipped two controls, and the shortfall was invisible because every row was true. **Truth is not the property a published table needs; earned-ness is.** The rows that were consequences of the two controls (the concurrent commands, the unrelated tenant) are fine — they are the same measurement stated twice. The one that composed the wait with the evaluation was a third claim wearing the first one's evidence.
- promotion: declined (the durable statement is the control itself and the sentence it earns in the book's authority chapter, which now says the rows are controls and names what the last one is falsified against).

## 2026-09-12 — The control I wrote for one defect found a second one I had not looked for

- This leaf's job was an absent ordering. I reused the shape that worked one leaf ago — hold the lock the *other* party would hold, and watch the handler underneath it — and it reproduced immediately. What I did not expect was the fourth control, the one I added almost as an afterthought because the handler visibly discarded its application error. Injecting a real SQL failure showed the endpoint answering `200 {"accepted":true}` while `node_events` stayed at zero rows.
- **The mechanism is worth naming because it is invisible at the call site.** PostgreSQL executes COMMIT on an aborted transaction as a ROLLBACK and reports success. So `if let Err(e) = … { log(e) } tx.commit()?` does not mean "commit what succeeded" — it means "report success for whatever the database decided to keep, which may be nothing". The project already knew this: `authority/transaction.rs` carries a `SELECT 1` probe with a comment saying exactly why. The knowledge existed in one transaction and not in the other.
- ⭐ **So the reusable lesson is not about this bug. It is that a defence written into an abstraction does not travel to the code that did not adopt the abstraction.** The guard runner is the safe path; `events` is a hand-rolled transaction that pre-dates it. Every such hand-rolled transaction is a place where a known hazard is unguarded by construction, and the way to find them is to grep for the *shape* the abstraction protects against — a discarded error before a commit — not to re-read the abstraction.
- The second thing I would keep is **how the ordering claim was observed**. "Guard before lease" is hard to assert directly: both orders look the same from outside, because the handler blocks either way. What separates them is whether the handler is *holding* the guard while it waits, so the control holds the LEASE, waits for `pg_stat_activity` to report a backend blocked on a `node_leases` lock, and then asks whether a concurrent exclusive guard acquisition still succeeds. Guard-first ⇒ it blocks. Lease-first ⇒ it sails through. One boolean, no sleeps, no timing tolerance.
- I also resisted a simplification that would have passed every control: taking the guard for the node's ENROLLMENT tenant instead of the inbox row's. It is one fewer read and it would be right almost always. But "almost always" is the wrong property for a lock: the guard has to name the tenant whose authority the effect will be evaluated against, and the only row that knows that is the one the effect uses. Binding the effect's own SQL to the guarded tenant makes the two agree by construction rather than by assumption.
- The honest reporting change is small and worth noticing: a dead-letter report for a command this node's ledger does not hold no longer increments the `dead_letters` counter. The old code counted a dead letter that had no row. Nobody would have found that from the counter; it fell out of asking "which row makes this event tenant-bound?"
- promotion: declined (the durable statements are the two controls, the `SELECT 1` probe's own comment at the call site, and the book's node-channel chapter, which now separates a rejected result from a failed one where a reader looks for it).

## 2026-09-12 — My control failed for the right reason and the wrong cause

- The first run of this leaf's reproduction printed exactly the failure message I had written, and I almost banked it. The number beside it said `event rows 0 -> 0`. The command had not completed while holding off a guard — it had been rejected at deserialization, because I posted a bare body where the API takes a command envelope. It "completed" without reaching any lock, which is indistinguishable from the defect if you read only the verdict.
- **A control that can fail for two different causes needs an assertion that separates them, and the separating assertion is almost always about the EFFECT rather than the timing.** "Did it finish early?" was satisfied by both stories. "Did it write an event row?" was satisfied by only one. I had put that assertion in for a different reason — to say what the command did while unordered — and it is what saved the leaf.
- The reproduction shape is the part I would reuse. The defect is an absent ordering, and the instinct is to spawn two racers and hope for an interleaving, which is slow, flaky, and proves a window rather than its absence. Holding the guard the *other* party would hold turns it into a deterministic observation: if the command completes underneath an exclusive guard, there is no ordering to narrow. No sleeps, no retries, no luck.
- On the repair, I deliberately did not wrap a 246-line function with 17 `?` uses in a boxed closure to adopt the guard runner. Factoring out the runner's own per-key acquisition gave both entrypoints the identical lock for a handful of lines. The rule I applied: **when integration is expensive, look for the primitive the machinery already uses before restructuring the caller around the machinery.**
- That said, the extracted helper is genuinely more dangerous than the runner, so it says so in its own doc comment: it takes one key, and a caller needing several must use the runner or lose the key ordering that prevents lock inversion. A convenience extracted from a safe abstraction usually drops an invariant, and the honest thing is to name which one at the extraction point rather than in a task record nobody reads at the call site.
- Choosing Shared over Exclusive mattered more than it looks. Exclusive would have "worked" — every control would pass — and would have serialized every command in a tenant behind every other. The mode is the difference between a fence and a global lock, and the control that proves it is the *passing* one, not the failing one.
- promotion: declined (the durable forms are `acquire_in_tx`'s own bounded doc comment and the two controls; the ordering contract is in the book's authority chapter where a reader looks for it).

## 2026-09-12 — A published census without its instrument is a number you cannot re-take

- The leaf said "repeat writer/caller census". I nearly repeated it by writing a fresh script from the recorded table and comparing totals. That would have produced 39-vs-42 and an confident story about migration — built on a predicate I had reconstructed from prose, which is exactly the shape `docs/CLAIM_VERIFICATION.md` warns about: a check and the thing it checks sharing a parent, agreeing, and carrying no information.
- What made it real was making the new script reproduce the OLD numbers first. All four — file count, byte count, corpus SHA-256, hit count — at the baseline commit. That single run is what converts "my number differs from theirs" into "the source changed", and it costs one command.
- So the deliverable turned out to be the instrument, not the number. `.3.3.4.1` did careful work and published a table and a hash; what it did not publish was the thing that produced them. **A measurement whose producer is not tracked can be read but not re-taken**, and every later leaf that needs to compare has to choose between trusting it and rebuilding it badly.
- The per-file diff mattered more than I expected. The total moved by −3, which reads like "three things migrated". The truth was two unrelated events: a module split moving three calls with net zero change, and three call sites of a different function disappearing entirely. A total would have merged them, and either story alone would have been wrong.
- **Two of this leaf's four deliverables came back "already done", and that is a legitimate result I had to resist dressing up.** There are no obsolete bridges to remove, and the ownership table needs no correction. Writing "measured; nothing to do" is more useful than manufacturing a change to justify the leaf — but it is only useful if the measurement is shown, which is why the 86/10/all-tests-functions breakdown is in the record rather than the conclusion alone.
- I deliberately did not edit `tenant-authority-paths.md` to match today's numbers. It is a historical measurement at its own baseline and this record is the comparison against it; rewriting it would have destroyed the thing that makes the comparison meaningful. The same instinct that says "keep the docs current" wants to update it, and that instinct is wrong for evidence.
- promotion: declined (the durable form is `scripts/census_authority_paths.py` itself, whose header carries the baseline reproduction as its own check — an instrument that cannot be forgotten in the way a method statement can).

## 2026-09-12 — I routed a finding out with a severity I had not measured

- One leaf ago I wrote this finding down as "an inconsistency… not a reachable escalation by a third party" and moved on. That judgement was reasoned from reading `canonical_server` and counting call sites, and it was defensible. It was also a severity claim I had not tested. The reproduction took ten minutes and showed the request going out with `authorization: Basic b3BlcmF0b3I6c2VjcmV0`.
- The severity did not actually change — it is still the operator's own configuration — but **what I knew about it did**. "The transport probably ignores userinfo" and "the transport converts userinfo into credentials and sends them" are different facts, and I had written the routing note as though the first were established.
- The lesson is narrower and more useful than "measure more": **when routing a finding OUT, the severity travels with it, and a severity is a claim.** A leaf that receives a routed finding inherits whatever confidence the routing note expressed. Mine said "inconsistency", which is a word that invites deprioritisation.
- Asserting on what the ORIGIN recorded rather than on the client's error message is the part of this control I would reuse. A refusal's own text tells you what the client believes it did; only the peer can tell you what actually left the machine. For anything shaped "nothing was sent", the assertion belongs on the far side.
- I also made `ApiClient::new` crate-private rather than leaving a checked and an unchecked constructor side by side in the public surface. Two constructors where one validates is an invitation, and the unchecked one has exactly one legitimate caller — the path that already validated.
- ⚠️ A scripted application of these edits failed halfway: an `&&` chain broke at a heredoc, so one script ran that should not have and left a `__SUITE__` placeholder in the tree. It was caught by grepping for the placeholder before committing. The pattern that saved the rest is the one already in these scripts — **assert every anchor before writing any file** — so the script that could not find its anchor wrote nothing at all rather than half of it. That is the same defence that `.11.4.5.2` exists to enforce, arriving from the other direction.
- promotion: declined (the durable form is the checked constructor plus the crate-private unchecked one — a shape that cannot be forgotten — and the control that measures the wire).

## 2026-09-12 — A test that hangs is not a test that fails, and a pipe ate an exit code

- The baseline control for this leaf does not go red on unrepaired production. It **hangs**, which is a different observation and needs different machinery: an outer `tokio::time::timeout` that converts "never returned" into a recorded failure with a duration attached. I nearly wrote the control without one and would have had a test that simply never finished — indistinguishable, in CI, from a stuck runner.
- Choosing the bound was the part I had to resist doing by feel. 60 s is not a round number I liked; it is twice the worst legitimate case, and the worst legitimate case is derivable from the server's own published budget — 15 s for its whole operation, and a caller can queue behind another caller's operation before running its own. Writing that derivation down matters more than the number, because the next person to change it needs to know what it was protecting.
- **The cost is real and I decided not to hide it.** Two controls each wait out the full 60 s, adding ~130 s to every CLI test run. The two ways to make that go away are both worse: lowering the bound would make the ceiling about test convenience rather than about the server's budget, and marking the controls `ignore` would mean the defect returns the first time nobody runs them by hand. So the cost is stated in the leaf as a deliberate charge.
- ⚠️ **A process hazard that nearly cost me a false green.** I ran the suite as `cargo test ... | grep -E "test result|Running|error"` in the background. A test FAILED, and the command reported **exit code 0** — because the pipeline's status is the last stage's, and `grep` succeeded. I only noticed because I read the filtered lines rather than the exit code. Piping a test runner through a filter discards the one signal that cannot be misread; if I want both, the runner's status has to be captured before the pipe (`cmd > file; rc=$?; grep file`), which is what the repeat runs now do.
- That same run then failed once and passed on every repeat, and I did NOT get to call it flaky and move on. Two hypotheses fit — a timing-sensitive control under load, or a stale test binary from the injection I had just reverted — and they are not separable by reasoning, only by repetition. Which is the same lesson as the last three leaves, arriving through a different door.
- The endpoint census produced a finding I deliberately did not fix here: 1 of 23 client constructions validates the configured base. Folding it in would have meant a constructor signature change at 22 call sites inside a leaf about timeouts. It is tracked with its census and its honest severity — the input is the operator's own configuration, so it is an inconsistency rather than a third-party escalation, and saying so is more useful than implying otherwise to justify the work.
- promotion: declined (the durable forms are the named constants with their derivation in the source, and the four controls; a knowledge card would restate the values without the reasoning that fixes them).

## 2026-09-12 — The gate refused the commit that introduced it, and it was right

- I wrote the LOCKSTEP box for this leaf saying `docs/TASK_TREE.md` was deliberately not restaged, because its row had been corrected four commits earlier and I had not touched it since. Then the new gate refused the commit: closing the leaf moved the tree's row 1, and the index was still pointing at the leaf being closed. **The identical shape as the defect the leaf exists to fix**, produced by me, in the act of fixing it, and caught on the gate's first live run.
- That is the strongest evidence a gate can produce, and it is also a warning about my own reasoning: I had checked the index, found it correct, and written down that it needed nothing — without noticing that the very edit I was making would invalidate it. "I verified X" has a timestamp, and mine was before the change.
- I corrected the box in place rather than deleting the sentence. A checklist that quietly loses a wrong claim reads the same as one that never made it.
- **The census rejected both options the leaf had written down, and I nearly skipped it because the answer felt obvious.** The leaf said "prefer the generator"; the measurement says a generator would destroy accurate prose in 13 of 14 rows, and that blanket equality would flag `PHASE-8`'s legitimately different cell. Three leaves in a row now where the census changed the repair — `.11.4.5.2`'s blanket rule, `.7.3.3.5`'s narrowing option, and this generator. The pattern is consistent enough to state plainly: **a rule proposed before its population is measured is wrong more often than not in this codebase.**
- Dating the drift was worth the extra minute. "It drifted" is a fact; "it drifted for 39 commits beginning at the commit that closed the leaf it named" is a mechanism, and the mechanism is what tells you the failure happens at SUCCESS — which is why nobody was looking.
- The rule covers one row today. I considered that an argument against building it, and it is the opposite: it is the only row that moves, and a rule that covers the moving part is worth more than one that covers thirteen static ones.
- promotion: declined (the durable form is the registered gate and its `DOCTRINE_ENFORCEMENT.md` row carrying the census; a knowledge card would restate what the check enforces).

## 2026-09-12 — Make the acceptance executable and it stops being an argument

- The leaf's acceptance was written as "must FAIL against `6bf0c40` and PASS against `c6a843f`". That is a precise sentence and I could have satisfied it by reasoning about the two diffs. Adding an `--against <sha>` mode turned it into two commands, and the difference is not convenience: a mode that replays the predicate over history is the only way to check a gate against the defect that motivated it once that defect is in the past.
- It also exposed something I would otherwise have reported wrongly. `c6a843f` passes — but it passes **vacuously**, because its LOCKSTEP mentions are narrative prose about a past box, not a bold claim. "Passes" and "was tested" are different facts. Running the gate over 30 commits and splitting them — 11 exercised, 19 vacuous — is the honest report; "30 green" would have been technically true and misleading, the exact shape `docs/CLAIM_VERIFICATION.md` warns about when a search returns a population and the author publishes it as a count.
- **The refusal text needed a line I did not expect to write.** The cheapest way to satisfy this gate is to delete the document's name from the box, and that is strictly worse than the defect: the box becomes true and the reader loses the record of what the leaf decided. So the refusal says so explicitly. A gate that can be satisfied by deleting the claim needs to say out loud that deleting the claim is not the remedy.
- I took the document list from COMMIT.md rather than from what I remembered, and then had the `--self-test` re-check that each name still appears there. That is cheap insurance against the exact drift this session already found twice — a fact copied out of its source and then maintained separately.
- One small thing worth keeping: the self-test caught my own arithmetic. I asserted a heading section for line 9 of an 8-line probe, and the test said so instead of quietly passing. A self-test whose expectations are hand-computed needs to fail loudly when the hand-computation is wrong, which is an argument for asserting exact values rather than "is non-empty".
- promotion: declined (the durable form is the registered gate itself, which cannot be forgotten, plus its row in `DOCTRINE_ENFORCEMENT.md` carrying the census; a knowledge card would restate what the check now enforces).

## 2026-09-12 — A widening is only as bounded as the control that tries to exceed it

- The decision listed four things the repair may not do. Writing them was easy; the honest question is whether any of them is *enforced* or merely intended. Two of the four turned out to be checkable cheaply, and I should have reached for both before writing the code rather than after.
- `git grep -n "fetch_admitting"` returning **exactly one call site** is the whole argument that R0-ranked acquisitions are untouched. That is a census, it took seconds, and it is stronger than the paragraph of reasoning it replaced. A widened entrypoint's blast radius is its caller list; enumerate it and paste the enumeration.
- The other bound — "no type the ranked pack does not advertise" — needed an injection, because grep cannot see it. Adding `application/json` to the admitted set and watching the unadvertised document acquire is the only way to know the negative control was ever load-bearing. It was. Without that run I would have shipped a negative assertion that happened to be true and might have stopped being true silently.
- **This repair flips a fact I published two commits ago.** `.7.3.3.4.1`'s control recorded `application/atom+xml` as `media_type_refused`, and now it acquires. The instinct is to quietly rewrite the earlier record; the discipline is that the old measurement is the *evidence the repair rests on*, so it stays where it was made and the new one says what superseded it. A project that erases its superseded measurements cannot show why it changed anything.
- The `SniffedKind::DeclaredType` variant bothered me until I wrote down why its `as_str` cannot be reached: the variant is only produced FROM a declared content type, so the fallback that uses `as_str` never fires for it. Writing "this is unreachable" without the *reason it is unreachable* is how a dead branch becomes a live one two refactors later. The reason went in the doc comment, and the live control asserts the observable consequence — the snapshot records the declared type, not a stand-in.
- One process note: a scripted injection asserted its anchor and *failed to apply*, and the suite then ran green. For a moment that green run looked like a falsification that had failed to falsify. The assert is what saved it. An injection that silently no-ops and leaves a green suite is the worst possible outcome of this technique, because it reads as evidence of robustness.
- promotion: declined (the durable contract is `docs/decisions/2026-09-12_r2-acquisition-accept-set.md`, which this child implements without changing it; the caller-census habit is already the `GAP-CLAIM-CENSUS` gate's subject).

## 2026-09-12 — The census changed the repair, which is the only reason to run one first

- I wrote the finding down two commits ago as "the R2 pack advertises five formats its acquisition leg refuses". I believed it, it was true, and the repair it implies — narrow the advertisement, or teach the leg those five formats — is the repair I would have written. Then I measured the untyped path and found the leg does not reason about formats at all: an all-printable body passes whatever format it is, and one NUL fails it. The rule is about transport-declared types and printability.
- That reframing kills one of the two candidate repairs outright. **There is no subset of the advertisement the leg accepts**, so narrowing the registry row cannot express the truth; narrowing to what it does accept would advertise `text/*`, which is the R0 pack's row, and would make the extraction pack rank for documents needing no extraction. I would not have known that from the sentence I wrote; I would have known it only after implementing the wrong half.
- **This is what "census before rule" buys, and it is the second time in three commits.** `.11.4.5.2` measured a LOCKSTEP rule that turned out to flag four innocent commits and miss the defect. Here the measurement deleted an option. In both cases the reasoned version was confident and wrong in a way no amount of re-reading would have caught.
- I was careful about what the census does NOT establish, because the tempting over-claim was right there. It measures the predicate, not files. "A PDF is refused" is not a fact — it depends on the PDF. What IS a fact: ZIP and tar cannot pass, because their formats begin with fixed-width NUL-padded structures, and that is reasoning over a measured predicate rather than a measurement of real files. I labelled it that way in the record.
- The census controls pass on unchanged production, which felt wrong to write until I named why: a census is not a regression test. Its value is that it *enumerates*, and that a later change to the accept set fails it. The falsification is what makes a passing census trustworthy, and it is cheap — one arm added to a match, one run, one revert.
- promotion: promoted → `docs/decisions/2026-09-12_r2-acquisition-accept-set.md`, three indexed answers.

## 2026-09-12 — Asserting the error kind would have been the comfortable mistake

- The leaf's acceptance said "the absence is asserted by count over the exact reference, **not inferred from a non-200**". My first instinct was still to lean on `acquisition_error.kind == "extraction_source_mismatch"` as the real assertion and treat the counts as belt-and-braces. The injection that settled it was the one that kept the refusal's name and wrote a snapshot before reporting it: the kind assertion passed, cheerfully, and only the count caught it. A handler that persists a foreign document and then complains about it is exactly the defect this leaf exists to prevent, and the obvious assertion is blind to it.
- That generalises past this leaf. **An assertion on an error's NAME tests the classifier; an assertion on state tests the effect.** When a refusal's whole value is that something did not happen, the name is a description of intent and the count is the fact.
- Three counts, not one, because each alone permits something different: the exact reference misses a row written under another id; the derivations join is trivially satisfied when there are no snapshots; the whole-table delta is the one that actually holds in a purged database. Writing all three took a minute and the third is the only one I would keep if forced to choose.
- The stub reply is well-formed on purpose, and it is worth saying why: a malformed reply is refused by the parser, which is a *different* refusal on a *different* path, and a control built on it would have exercised the JSON decoder while claiming to exercise the digest binding. A dishonest input has to be honest in every respect except the one under test.
- `R2_WORKER_BIN` is process-wide, which is the kind of shared mutable state that produces a defect in someone else's test next month. The suite's `guard()` serializes this file and the override is removed before any assertion, but I note it as a hazard rather than a solved problem — the existing `extraction_input.rs` controls needed a dedicated mutex for exactly this, after one of them was handed a sibling's worker.
- The third defect of this session's own making turned up while updating the frontier: `docs/TASK_TREE.md`'s frontier column still named a leaf closed on 2026-09-11. Same shape as the other two — a fact copied into a second document that nothing derives. The project already fixed this once for the book, in `check_book_frontier.sh`, and simply did not extend it to its own index. Opened as `.11.4.5.3`, with the generator preferred over another checker: removing the copy beats detecting its staleness.
- The enforcer then caught me writing "a second copy nothing gates" into the frontier table with no census behind it — a sentence quantifying over the whole repository, stated because it felt obviously true. It *was* true, which is the uncomfortable part: `git grep -n "TASK_TREE" -- scripts/ .githooks/` hits five files and classifying them leaves zero that read the column. Thirty seconds to measure, and I had been about to ship it as an impression. The gate did its job on its author.
- promotion: declined (the durable forms are the leaf's recorded falsification table and `.11.4.5.3`'s acceptance; a knowledge card would restate what the control now enforces).

## 2026-09-12 — A control that only ever passes, and the gate the design forgot

- The leaf arrived with its design pre-established, which was genuinely useful — `from_config` public, `classify` reaching `allow_ip` before any resolver call, the Atom feed as the served document. It was also wrong in a way that would have produced a control passing on false data. `resolvers::resolve` filters on the registry row's `schemes @> [reference.scheme]`; migration 0027 declares `["https"]`; so an honest `http` reference ranks no R2 pack and never reaches the arm under test.
- The trap: `resource_references.scheme` is caller-supplied and is **not** validated against the locator. Writing `scheme: "https"` next to `original_locator: "http://…"` would have compiled, resolved, acquired, and produced a green control asserting real digests — over a reference whose stored scheme is a lie. It would have proved the pipeline works for a routing case that cannot occur. The registry row is the honest lever because it is a deployment fact, and it is restored before any assertion so no later suite in the same database inherits it.
- **The falsification is where the leaf earned its result, not the green run.** Serving one extra byte failed the raw-digest assertions — and left the three chunk digests *byte-identical*, because a trailing newline does not change a parsed feed. Had I written only the derivation assertions, which felt like the richer check, the control would have been green on a document it had not actually verified. Two legs, because they fail on different things: the raw bytes catch what the parser normalises away, the chunks catch what the handler mangles after parsing.
- Neutering `with_acquisition` so it discards the supplied fetcher was the cheapest and most informative injection of the three: it answers "is the seam load-bearing, or would this pass anyway?" in one run. Any seam added for a test should be asked that question once.
- **The finding was not on the task list, and reading was what found it.** Tracing why `application/atom+xml` could not be served, I hit `sniff_kind`: a declared content type is accepted only when it is `text/html`, `application/xhtml+xml` or `text/*`. Migration 0027 advertises five types, none of them in that set. So the pack is ranked for documents its own acquisition leg refuses — the caller gets an acquisition refusal where §12.2 promises `resource_unresolvable_now`. That is a wrong answer, not a gap.
- I stopped short of the tempting second half. The source reads as though an untyped PDF or ZIP would fail the byte-sniff's UTF-8/control-character test, and it would have been easy to write that down as fact. It is a hypothesis; `.7.3.3.5` owns measuring it per format. The measured half — one format, live, on the same bytes that succeed under a different type — is worth more than five formats of confident reading.
- The control refuses rather than skipping when the extraction worker is absent. The suite's convention is a printed SKIP, and I followed it elsewhere, but a control whose entire purpose is closing a stated coverage gap must not be able to report neither way: a green suite that silently did not run it is exactly how the gap survived a phase closure in the first place.
- promotion: declined (the durable forms are the leaf's recorded falsification table and `.7.3.3.5`'s acceptance; a knowledge card would restate what the control now enforces).

## 2026-09-12 — "Recorded" is not "fixed", and I needed to be told

- I found two defects in my own work, wrote both up honestly, and shipped. The director asked the obvious question: recorded, or fixed? One was fixed-in-instance with the trap left armed; the other was a paragraph of prose in a repository whose entire thesis is that a rule living only in a doc is a suggestion. Writing the lesson down felt like closure. It was not.
- The registry defect was worse than its symptom. Words vanishing from a description is cosmetic; the driver **executing** whatever a description contains, on every commit and in CI, is a small code-execution surface in the one component everything else depends on. The fix has to run BEFORE the array is assigned, because by the time bash has parsed the assignment the execution has already happened — a guard that inspects the expanded array would report a breach it had already suffered.
- The census saved me from shipping a bad rule. My first instinct for the LOCKSTEP defect was "closing a leaf must stage MEMORY.md". Measured over 25 commits: MEMORY is staged in 6 of 10 leaf-closing commits. That rule would have asserted something the project does not do, flagged four innocent commits, and *still* missed the defect I was fixing, which did stage CHANGELOG. The rule that survives is keyed on the author's own claim — 1 breach in 6, zero false positives — and I would not have found it by reasoning.
- The failure that produced all of this is worth keeping in one sentence: **a scripted doc edit that fails must fail the commit, and `git add -A` after a partially-failed script is how a false checklist box gets written.** It happened again while writing this leaf — an assertion fired mid-script — and this time nothing was written, because the write is the last statement. That ordering is the whole defence.
- promotion: declined (the durable forms are the driver's own self-guard, which cannot be forgotten, and `.11.4.5.2`'s recorded census and acceptance for the half that is still open; a knowledge card would restate what the gate now enforces).

## 2026-09-12 — The lever worth pulling was not on the list

- Two levers had been named for the checkpoint's cost, and both were about making the gate cheaper. Both turned out to be wrong, and for different reasons: the macOS setting trades away validation of exactly the untrusted-content workers this project builds, and it cannot be committed — a fix no other machine inherits is a local secret that makes the published number false everywhere else. The volume move died on a measurement I nearly did not take: the repository is 3.77 MiB, but its build tree is 185 GB against 249 GB of free boot volume. Feasible for the repo, not for the tree that has to follow it.
- The lever that worked was a different question. Not "how do I make this gate cheaper" but "which gate is authoritative" — and the answer was already running: the remote does all eight commands, two of them more strictly, from a cold checkout, in a tenth of the time. I verified that from the workflow files rather than from the prose describing them, which matters, because the prose is what I would have quoted.
- Naming the trade is part of the decision, not a caveat on it. Moving the gate remote makes gate latency depend on the push cadence, and I did not change the cadence — it was not mine to change, and saying so is cheaper than discovering later that someone assumed I had.
- The uncomfortable part: my own previous commit claimed a LOCKSTEP it did not have. A scripted edit hit a failed assertion, the script died, `git add -A` staged whatever had succeeded, and the commit went through with a checklist box that was false. The gate cannot catch that — it checks that the box cites something, not that the edit landed. The fix is procedural and I am writing it down because I will hit it again: **a scripted doc edit that fails must fail the commit, and `git add -A` after a partially-failed script is exactly how a false checklist box gets written.** Verify the edit took, in the same breath as making it.
- promotion: declined (the durable statements are the decision record and COMMIT.md's push-cadence section — the place a reader actually looks before pushing; a knowledge card would be a third copy of a rule that now lives in the workflow doc).

## 2026-09-12 — The census found a defect the leaf was not looking for

- I opened the leaf for one thing — five leaves whose status contradicts itself — and the instrument I wrote to count them returned an absurd number: one section holding 18 status lines. The temptation was to fix the regex and move on. The absurd number was the finding: the tree uses `#######` and deeper, Markdown stops at 6, and those lines are not headings at all. 29 of them. The deepest leaves of the project's primary work-memory file were invisible as structure to anything that parses headings.
- That is worth stating as a rule: **when an instrument returns a number that cannot be right, the instrument is usually fine and the corpus is wrong.** I nearly "fixed" a correct regex.
- Both remedies preserve everything. `- Opened:` is what the stale line always meant, and the leaf id carries depth better than hashes ever did — the identifier `TREE.3.3.4.3.3.3.3.2.3.2` is ten levels of hierarchy in a string that renders anywhere.
- Then I broke my own gate while writing it. The registry rows are bash double-quoted strings; my backticks became command substitution, the enforcer printed `pending: command not found`, and the description rendered with words missing. It was in the output the whole time. Reading tool output rather than skimming for the green tick is the only reason it did not ship.
- promotion: declined (the durable form of both lessons is the registry itself — they are enforced now, not remembered, and `DOCTRINE_ENFORCEMENT.md` carries the measured rationale).

## 2026-09-12 — Find out whose seconds they are before you shrink the gate

- An hour-long checkpoint invites exactly one reflex: cut something. The honest first move is to ask where the hour goes, and the answer here was not in the gate at all — it was macOS validating each freshly written executable on its first run, ~21.9s apiece on this volume and ~0.15s on the boot volume.
- Both cheap instruments beat the expensive reasoning. The Linux runner had already run the same four commands and its own log said 444s with 4.2% unaccounted, from a COLD checkout — an oracle I did not build and did not have to pay for. Then copying one binary to each volume and timing its first execution settled the cause in ninety seconds. I had been composing an argument about `--all-features` feature unification; the experiment made the argument unnecessary.
- The leaf's own leading candidate was wrong, and it was right to say so out loud. Nine rustdoc doctest-harness builds sounded plausible and cost at most 8%. A candidate recorded honestly as a candidate is exactly what let the measurement replace it without embarrassment.
- The deliverable turned out to be a NUMBER, not a repair: one more integration-test file costs about 22 seconds of every future checkpoint. That converts the next throughput argument from taste into arithmetic, which is the whole point of measuring.
- The uncomfortable part is worth stating: this is the first evidence that §13's storage-locality policy has a real, large, previously invisible cost on this machine. The policy is sound and I am not proposing to weaken it — but the director should know the price, and the two levers that could change it are theirs, not mine.
- promotion: accepted — `TOOLBOX.md`, "A slow gate is a measurement, not a verdict on the gate".

## 2026-09-12 — A control that is host-dependent is telling you something

- The navigation-deadline control passed on the runner and failed on this machine, and nothing in the code under test was host-specific. The tempting reading is "flaky test, widen the assertion". The actual reading is that the field it asserted was written from two independent facts, so every assertion about it was silently a conjunction — including a clause about host speed that the author never meant to write.
- The product had the same defect the control did. A caller reading `kind` to learn why ITS request failed was handed an operator's fact about a possibly-live process. One field, two audiences, and when they disagreed it served neither.
- The doctrine tension was real and worth settling rather than dodging: the rule is never to CLAIM an unobserved termination. But that rule is satisfied by CARRYING the cleanup fact, not by destroying the render's. The old code satisfied it by destruction, which is one way, and a lossy one. Exactly one corner needed the old behaviour kept — a SUCCESSFUL render under unconfirmed cleanup, where the refusal is load-bearing rather than a label. Finding that corner before changing anything is the part that took the thinking.
- The instrument again beat the fix in value. A load-dependent trigger is not a control: it did not fire once on a quiet machine. Injecting the SHAPE — a forked child that calls `setsid` and holds the inherited stderr — reproduces it in twelve seconds on any host with no browser at all. The slow host and the fast host are now both controls that run every time, instead of one that gets waited for.
- Two claims in the filed finding did not survive re-derivation: a receipt was attributed to the wrong producer, and "it fails identically warm" was contradicted by a passing run. Neither changed the repair, and both are worth the correction — a finding's narrative is evidence about what its author saw, not about what is true.
- promotion: accepted — `docs/knowledge/one-field-cannot-carry-two-facts.md`.

## 2026-09-12 — A passing suite is not evidence that a race is closed

- The stub `ETXTBSY` came back, and the earlier repair's comment had said in as many words that it left "no window at all". It did not. One `OnceLock` per adapter kind serialises each stub against itself and against nothing else, and the descriptor that leaks into a `fork` belongs to a DIFFERENT stub than the one failing to exec. I wrote that comment, and I believed it because the suite went green.
- That is the part worth keeping. A claim that a race is closed is a claim about what CANNOT happen, and no number of passing scenarios establishes it — least of all on a platform that does not enforce the rule being violated. macOS cannot produce `ETXTBSY` at any timing, so every local run was silent by construction.
- So this repair asserts the INVARIANT instead: holding either stub path must imply every stub is already written and executable. That control fails against the superseded design while all three conformance scenarios still pass — which is precisely the shape of the original mistake, now caught on the development machine instead of on the runner.
- Third time today that the durable fix was an instrument rather than a code change: suppress ambient git config, remove the warm `target/`, assert the invariant. The pattern underneath all three is that the development platform is silent about the thing that fails, so the test has to be written against the CAUSE rather than the effect.
- promotion: accepted — `docs/knowledge/proving-a-race-is-closed.md`.

## 2026-09-11 — Checking the thing I was about to "repair on principle"

- I had carried a note that the extract test helper shared the inode-reuse weakness REPAIR-0079 fixed, and was about to open a leaf for it. It does not. It holds an open `File` for the owner's life and compares the path against that LIVE descriptor plus `nlink == 1` — the same shape as the repaired production owner. The weak form was comparing against a `Metadata` captured at creation with the descriptor already closed, which is a different thing that happens to look similar in a grep.
- Second false entry I have had to correct in a durable record today, both the same class: a claim carried forward from an earlier reading instead of re-derived. The first was a "still open" remote failure list the runner had already cleared. Writing it down does not make it true later.
- The measurement, for the record: on APFS a successor at the same path never reused the inode in either case — 0 of 300 with the descriptor held, 0 of 300 with it closed. So this machine cannot tell the sound shape from the unsound one, which is exactly why the unsound one survived here until ext4 exposed it. A probe that cannot fail is not evidence, and I should say so when reporting one.
- promotion: accepted — `docs/knowledge/proving-a-path-still-names-what-you-created.md`, which answers the identity question for both files and directories and records the platform's blindness to it.
- And promoting it found a defect in the promotion machinery itself. The LESSON-PROMOTION gate offers `docs/knowledge/<slug>.md` as one of its two remedies, the Knowledge Map generator scanned only `subsystems.md`, `docs/tasks/` and `docs/decisions/`, and `docs/knowledge/` did not exist. So that remedy produced a record no one could find by question — **precisely the defect the gate exists to stop**, reachable by following the gate's own instructions. The gate went green on a promotion that had not actually promoted anything.
- Worth stating plainly: a gate that checks for the ARTIFACT of a remedy rather than the EFFECT of it can certify its own failure mode. I only caught it because I checked whether the map listed the file, which the gate never asked me to do. The generator now indexes `docs/knowledge/` and prints each record's `answers:` line, so the map is searchable by question rather than filename.

## 2026-09-11 — The branch that only runs on a stranger's machine

- The `pg_guard` parent creation had a race I could reproduce on this machine 346 times in 640 tries, and it had never once failed a local test run. Both facts are true and the reconciliation is the whole lesson: the racing branch is guarded by `if !parent.exists()`, and after the first local run the parent always exists. The branch is dead code here and live code on every fresh checkout.
- So the defect was not Linux-only, not timing-only, and not environmental in any interesting way. It was **warm-state-only-invisible**. My local suite was not testing the code CI was running.
- That is the second time today. The git fixtures read the developer's ambient identity and could not have passed on any clean machine; this one cannot fail on any dirty one. Both were reproduced locally within minutes once I asked what my machine HAS that the runner lacks, rather than what the runner has that my machine lacks. I had the question backwards for most of the day, and paid for it in CI round-trips.
- Worth being precise about what remains genuinely remote: `ETXTBSY`, the 108-byte `sun_path` limit, and inode reuse are real kernel differences a macOS box cannot exhibit at any state. Accumulated local state is not in that category, and conflating the two is what made "remote-only" feel like a bigger class than it is. Promoted the distinction to `TOOLBOX.md`.
- promotion: accepted — `TOOLBOX.md` gains a section and a cold-tree probe row.

## 2026-09-11 — The cleanup that only ran on failure

- Repairing the acquisition's colliding name, I found the defect the census had not: the success path removed nothing. The doc comment said "the caller owns its cleanup", and there is no production caller — only the receipt, which reads the digest and walks away. So every successful acquisition left a bare repository in the ambient temporary directory forever, and only FAILURES were cleaned up. A contract that names an owner who does not exist is not a contract.
- That is the more interesting half. The collision was a real defect and the probe measured it brutally — 501 distinct names in 2000 calls, every collision adjacent — but it needed concurrency to bite. The leak needed nothing at all; it happened on every success since the code was written, and no test noticed because every test cleaned up after itself by hand.
- The measured surprise: an open descriptor pins a directory's inode (0 reuses in 200 cycles), but macOS still reports `nlink == 2` on that descriptor AFTER the directory is removed. The link-count trick that REPAIR-0079 relied on for FILES carries no signal for directories, and I would have shipped it as a check that silently never fires had I assumed the file case generalized. Two owners, two identity proofs, for a reason.
- The shared module is the other half of the lesson. `extraction_input` had already proved every storage rule this needed — runtime root discovery, 0700 parents, checked ownership, exclusive creation. Duplicating them into git.rs would have produced two implementations that drift. One of them being right is not the same as the project being right.
- promotion: declined (an instance of the existing owned-storage rule; the durable additions are the directory-identity fact and the probe, both recorded).

## 2026-09-11 — The first remote-only failure class I could reproduce without a remote

- Four `git::tests::*` failed on the runner with `the commit writes: AuthorMissing`. `gix`'s `repo.commit` resolves its signature from git configuration, so four fixture commits silently borrowed whatever identity the developer's machine carried. A clean runner has none.
- The important part is not the fix but the reproduction. `GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL=/dev/null GIT_CONFIG_SYSTEM=/dev/null GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM=1` recreates the runner's condition on this machine exactly, and it earned its keep immediately: after I repaired three sites and believed I was done, the probe failed with `5 passed; 1 failed` and named a FOURTH site I had missed. Without it I would have spent a full remote round-trip learning that.
- That is four Linux-or-clean-machine-only defects today — ETXTBSY, the singleton socket path, inode reuse, and this — and this is the first one an instrument could observe locally. The distinction worth keeping: ETXTBSY and inode reuse are genuine kernel differences that macOS cannot exhibit, but this one was never about Linux at all. It was about a machine without the developer's dotfiles. I had been treating "remote-only" as one category when it is two, and only one of them needs a remote.
- The generalizable rule: a test that reads ambient user configuration is not a test of the code, it is a test of the machine. The fixture now owns its identity the same way it owns its storage, under the reserved `.invalid` domain.
- promotion: declined (this is an instance of the existing fixture-ownership rule, now with a probe recorded in `TOOLBOX.md`).

## 2026-09-11 — Not libraries: a 228-byte path against a 108-byte limit
